## REVIEW

# O'Connor movement review

Read the complete supplied movement in chapter order before judging it. State
whether this is self-review, a fresh-context simulated cold read, or a real human
read. Do not claim independent testing when you have the drafting history.

First give an unscored reader response: where interest caught, where it slipped,
which person matters, which event you expect next, and whether you would continue.
Then use two perspectives: a fluent thirteen-year-old reader and an adult genre
reader. These are lenses, not demographic research. Do not infer a sales forecast.

For each applicable dimension give a 1–10 score, an exact passage location, a reason
and confidence. Mark an absent feature N/A instead of giving invented evidence:

| Dimension | What to judge |
|---|---|
| Opening pull | A concrete reason to care before explanation takes over |
| Keep reading | Unresolved pressure and satisfying local payoff |
| Interest / freshness | Specific people, choices and problems |
| Clarity / flow | Meaning on one pass and clean transitions |
| Character attachment | Agency, vulnerability, recognizable differences |
| Humor / warmth | Character-grounded pleasure, timing, emotional honesty |
| Action / suspense | Trackable contest, adaptation, cost, earned scene length |
| Progression payoff | Effort visibly changes capability and consequence |
| Connection | State, plants and relationships carried across chapters |
| Read-aloud quality | Punctuation, referents, dialogue attribution and cadence |

Anchor interpretation: 5 = understandable but inconsistent; 7 = engaging with
located weaknesses; 9 = compelling with few substantial distractions. These are
editorial judgments, not calibrated measurements. Do not average the two readers
into a release gate. Do not award points for matching another book's word counts.

Separately check canon, physical state, power limits, knowledge boundaries and
reserved disclosures. Cite the project evidence for a contradiction. If only the
plan supports a claim, identify it as plan adherence rather than a canon error.

Return a concise repair brief with at most three priorities. Each needs location,
observed issue, effect on the reader, proposed scope and a strength to preserve.
Retain the developed action the owner requested. Punctuation can repair an unclear
sentence; do not shorten a successful fight merely to reduce its word count.

If comparing runs, keep chapter coverage and scoring rubric equal. Hide model and
seat labels from a fresh reader where feasible. Report tradeoffs and uncertainty;
one movement does not prove which seat is better across a series.

## CHAPTER — books/book-2680-opening/manuscript/chapter-09.md

# Chapter 9 — Kiva: Dark Hands

Somebody had left a quench box in front of her peg.

It was a real one, a steel box about the size of a loaf with a hinged lid, and when Kiva lifted the lid with her good hand, she found it packed to the brim with wet grey salt. A paper tag hung from the handle on a loop of string. *For emergencies,* it said, in neat capitals that somebody had taken trouble over.

She stood and looked at it for a while.

It was a quarter to eight on Tuesday morning, and it was her first day as a rostered student of the Crooked Line School. She had been coming through the side gate for four years, to the junior lanes and to Orla's nights in the Old Yard, but she had never been allowed past the junior yard into the long brick range where the division teams kept their kit. The first team had a room on the upper floor with tall windows. The second team had the room beneath it. The third team had the Low Lane, which had been a cart shed once and stood at the bottom of the school against the wall of the core house, so that the whole room hummed very faintly all day, like a kettle that was thinking about boiling.

Sixteen pegs ran along the wall, each with a slate over it. The last slate said *K. ROWAN — PROV.* in fresh chalk.

"That's good salt," said a voice behind her.

She turned. The boy in the doorway was nineteen or twenty, long and tired-looking, with brown hair tied back in a string and a front tooth chipped off at a slant. He had a third-year's grey band on his sleeve and a kit bag over his shoulder, and he was studying the box at her feet the way a carpenter studies a plank somebody has left in the road.

"Proper quench salt. Wet through." He crouched, pressed a thumb into it, and sniffed the thumb. "The lane warden's been short since the winter. Do you want it?"

"It was left for me."

"I can see that. I'm asking if you want it." He glanced up. "Because if you don't, I'll put it under the mast where it belongs, and whoever wrote that tag will have given the Low Lane a present, which I expect will annoy them a good deal."

Kiva thought about it. Her face was hot. She made herself think about it anyway.

"Put it under the mast," she said.

"Good." He picked up the box, pulled off the tag, and held the tag out to her between two fingers. "Keep that, though. I kept mine. My first week somebody nailed a child's wooden sword over my peg." He stood. "Jude Talley. I'm the captain, which in Third Division means I'm the one who signs for the litter when it breaks. You're the provisional."

"Kiva."

"I know. Everybody knows. Sit down, Kiva. I've got to ask you something before the others come in, and I'd rather not do it standing over you."

---

She sat on the bench under her peg with the tag in her hand, and Jude Talley sat on an upturned bucket across from her and rested his elbows on his knees.

"I've been in Third for three years," he said. "I'm not going to tell you that's by choice. My Edge hold is six and a half on a good day and my rank's been Flame since I was sixteen, and neither of them has moved, and I've stopped waiting for them to. I'm useful. I'm not going up. I worked that out last spring and it was a bad month, and then it was over." He said it plainly, without asking her for anything. "So I'm not angry that you're here, and I'm not frightened of you. I want that understood, because half this school is one or the other and you'll get tired of guessing which."

"All right."

"What I am is careful. Your placement says one incident in a sanctioned event and you're off the roster. It doesn't say what happens to the event." He held up a finger. "I looked it up. The lane's result is voided and the team takes a nought. We have six meets this season. A nought in one of them is the difference between staying Third and being struck from the Circuit, and there are three other people on this team who need the tuition." He lowered the finger. "So I need to know exactly what you won't do. Not what you can do. I've read what you can do. It was on the Cellhouse board."

Kiva looked down at the tag. *For emergencies.*

Mara had asked her nearly the same thing across the kitchen table, six days ago, and she had found then that it was easier to say than she had expected. It was easier still the second time.

"I won't stack," she said. "Two doors or three, on purpose or because I'm losing. I can use one at a time and keep the others out, and I've done it under pressure for about two minutes. I won't open anything near a cell. I have to tell the referee which door I'm on before the bell, and if I change, I have to say so out loud first, and that's slow, and anyone listening will hear it." She breathed in. "The thing I can't promise is what happens if I'm frightened. That's what went wrong in the Cellhouse. I haven't learned it yet. I'll say when it's starting. That part I can do."

Jude was quiet. Outside, in the yard, somebody was dragging something heavy over the cobbles.

"That," he said at last, "is the most useful thing a teammate has told me in three years." He stood and picked up the quench box. "Most of them tell me what they're good at. Come on. I'll show you the lane, and then you can watch us be bad at carrying a sandbag. You can't do anything else with that arm, I suppose?"

"Not until Friday."

"Then you'll keep the clock. Somebody told me you can keep a clock."

---

The Low Lane's floor sloped.

It did not slope much, a hand's width from the door to the drain at the far end, but Kiva felt it through her boots the moment she stepped onto it, and she saw from the way Jude walked that he had stopped noticing years ago. The lane was thirty paces long and eight wide, floored in old brick, with a sensing mast in the corner that had a wooden case instead of a steel one and a paper drum behind a little glass door. A new tag was tied to the mast's handle. She did not have to read it to know whose name was on it, but she read it.

*Rowan, K. Door declared to lane warden on entry. Tape pulled nightly. By order, Placement Chair.*

"They pull everybody's tape once a week," said Jude, setting the salt down beneath it. "Yours they pull every night. I'd take it as a compliment. Nobody's wanted to read mine since I was a first-year."

"I want to read it," said someone else. "Hers, I mean. Not yours. Sorry, Jude."

There were two of them in the doorway. The girl was small and round-faced, with a great deal of fair hair escaping from a scarf, and she was carrying a litter pole in each fist and talking before she was properly through the door. "I knew the box would still be there. I said so. I said, she'll come in early because she's that sort, and she'll see it, and she'll cry or she'll leave, and then we'll be three for the Stonehand meet and they'll walk the litter over us like a carpet, and Jude said she won't cry, and I said how do you know—" She stopped, because she had seen Kiva. "Oh. You didn't cry."

"Not yet," said Kiva.

"That's Pru Finch," said Jude. "Hand door. She wards well and she narrates disasters before they happen, so if you ever want to know the worst thing that could come of a plan, ask Pru. And that's Noa."

The boy behind Pru was thin and dark, with ink on the side of his right hand and a strip of paper tape wound round and round his left like a bandage. He did not come in. He went straight to the mast, opened its little glass door, and peered at the drum.

"It hasn't moved," he said. "Since you walked in. I wondered if it would." He turned around. He had a narrow, serious face that looked as if it had been interrupted in the middle of a sum. "Noa Bexley. Ember door, Stride mostly, my Edge is a disgrace. I keep the team's tapes, because nobody else wanted to, and then I started reading them, and now I can't stop. Can I ask you something rude?"

"You can ask."

"Your draw at the bench was three point one. I looked at the board on Classification Day. But I pulled the Terrace Ring tape for your bout, and in the half after you stopped using two doors, your dram ran at about what Jude spends." He unwound a length of the tape from his hand and held it up to the window, as though the answer might be printed on it. "Those can't both be you. One of them's measuring something else. I'd like to know which." He caught Jude's look. "Not today. I know. I only wanted to say it before I forgot how I'd put it."

"He'll ask again," said Jude. "Every day. You're allowed to say no."

Kiva had been called a great many things at the draw bench in four years. Nobody had ever suggested that the bench might be the one that was wrong. She did not know what to do with it, so she put it away to look at later, the way she put away a part she could not yet find the place for.

"Ask me again on Friday," she said. "When I've got two arms."

---

The Cellhouse looked smaller in the afternoon, with no banners on it.

She went down the side stair at one o'clock, as the call sheet said, past a door marked *KENNEL — LICENSED CANDIDATES AND WREN HOUSE ONLY*, and came out into a long, low room that smelled of brick dust and wet salt. Benches ran across it in rows. The whole of one wall was glass, thick and slightly green, and beyond the glass and a story below it lay a floor she had never seen before.

It was the old refinery's settling hall. It ran twenty-four paces from end to end beneath a vaulted brick ceiling, and three drained tanks were sunk across the middle of it like three shallow graves, each a pace deep, six long and three wide, with a sloping bottom and an iron grate at the low end. Plank bridges crossed between them. At the far end stood a steel door with a second steel door behind it and a long lever beside the frame, and even from here, through the glass and the floor and her boots, she could feel what was behind that door, a slow steady pressure like a thumb resting on the back of her neck.

About fifty first-years were finding places on the benches. Team Seven had already found theirs, in the back row, and had left a gap at the end.

Perrin saw her first and raised both hands. They were still wrapped in white cloth from the knuckles to the wrists, and he waved them like a man signaling a tram. "The sling's here," he said. "We're complete. A boy with no hands, a girl with one arm, a girl with a list, and Kellan."

Kellan Renn sat at the far end with his back very straight and a First Division band on his sleeve, white on grey, so new that the stitching showed. He nodded to Kiva once. He did not look as if he had enjoyed the last ten minutes.

"He's angry," said Anwen quietly, as Kiva sat. She had her list on her knee. "Not at you. Read the wall."

A slate hung on the wall beside the glass, ruled in two columns. *FIELD LICENSE, FIRST STEP — CALL ORDER.* Forty team numbers ran down it, and beside each a trial score. The order had nothing to do with the numbers the teams wore. Team Two stood first. Team Thirty-One was sixth. She found her own at last, because there was nowhere left to look.

*40. Team Seven. Trial failed. Call last.*

"Order of trial score," said Kellan, without turning his head. "The first twelve were licensed on Sunday and Monday, out at the Ferren culverts. There was a tear, and the Home Line held it open for a day and a half. Twelve teams. Mine would have been one of them." He paused. "It's a fair rule. I'd have written it the same way."

"Doesn't make it taste better," said Perrin.

"No," said Kellan. "It doesn't."

---

The Kennel warden came in by a door beside the glass, and the room went quiet without being asked.

He was a thin, bald man of fifty-five or so in a Wren House apron, and his name, said the slate, was Oswin Druce. His fingertips were stained the grey of pencil lead, all ten of them, and when he spoke his voice was so soft that the back row leaned forward to catch it.

"You've all killed one," he said. "Or stood near somebody who did. In the Cellhouse, with the gallery watching and a clock running, and you thought that was the work." He set a wooden thing on the table in front of him. "That was the easy half. I'm here for the other."

The wooden thing was a husk, life-size, carved from some pale timber and painted grey. It had the low front end and the four thick stubby limbs and no face, and somebody had sawn it in two along its length and hinged it at the spine. Druce opened it like a book.

The inside was painted the same flat grey as the outside, all the way through. There were no lungs in it and no gut, no bones and no brain. There was only one thing that was not grey, a knot of red cord about the size of a fist, nailed into the wood at the place where the heart would be on a dog.

"There is nothing in it," said Druce. "I want you to carry that picture out of here if you carry nothing else. A husk is ash holding a shape, the same way the ash in a cell holds a tune, except that nobody in this world tuned it. Something on the far side of a tear pressed a pattern into loose ash and then went away, or was never there to begin with. I don't know which, and nor does anybody else, whatever they tell you at the Wren House after a glass of wine." He tapped the red cord. "What I know is where it's tied. We call that the knot. Put a lit Edge through the knot, and the shape lets go."

A hand went up near the front, from a Stonehand boy with a neck like a gatepost. "What if you take a leg off?"

"Then it grows another out of its own middle, and it's a little smaller, and you're a little more tired. The limbs are lent. Cut off all four and you'll have a small angry husk and a sore arm." A few people laughed. Druce did not. "It doesn't see. It reads. That front end turns toward the brightest worked power it can feel inside about twelve paces, which means a lit blade, or a ward, or a stance if you're near enough, or a cell if the cell is running hotter than you are. It goes in a straight line and it does not feint, and it does not remember you from one minute to the next. That is the last comfortable thing I'll say this afternoon."

He closed the wooden husk and looked along the benches.

"Now watch what happens after."

---

He went down to the floor himself.

Kiva watched through the green glass as he crossed the nearest plank bridge with a roll of canvas under one arm, and a second-year in a leather apron followed him, an Ember-door girl with her cuff already bare. He unrolled the canvas on the brick between two of the tanks. It was a waxed cloth about two paces square with a lead weight sewn into each corner, and he laid it down the way Mara laid out a felt before she opened a gauge, squarely, smoothing it with the side of his hand. Then he walked to the lever by the steel door and pulled it.

His voice came up a speaking tube beside the glass, thin and close. "One Handful. Mind where she kills it."

The inner door opened. The husk that came through was the same as every husk Kiva had ever seen, grey and low and hurried, and it swung its blind front end toward the girl's blade the instant she lit it. She had planted herself on the far side of the cloth. It came for her in a straight line, across the canvas, and she put her edge through it at the height of the red cord, once, without any fuss.

It let go.

Kiva had seen that a dozen times in the Cellhouse and had not once looked at it properly, because there had always been a needle to watch. She looked now. The husk did not drop. It poured, the whole shape slumping inward and down onto the cloth in a soft grey rush, and then the heap moved. The top of it lifted and settled, lifted and settled, as if something beneath were turning over in its sleep.

"One," said Druce's voice up the tube. "Two. Three."

The second-year had already backed away three long paces, and her blade had run home into her cuff. Her hands hung empty at her sides.

"That heap is open," said Druce. He was walking toward it, counting between his sentences, and his own hands were empty too. "Eight. Nine. For sixty counts, near enough, fresh ash has no owner and no tune and it is listening for one. Twelve. It'll take the first pattern that touches it. Watch."

He lifted a pole from a rack on the wall. A small lamp-cell hung from the end of it, lit, and he held it out at arm's length until the lamp was two paces from the heap.

A thread rose off the ash.

It was as fine as the smoke from a snuffed candle, and it leaned. There was no draft in that sealed brick hall to move it. It bent toward the lamp-cell like smoke toward a flue, and it kept bending, and on the side of the little cell a needle no longer than a fingernail began to shiver.

"Loose ash finds the nearest lamp," said Druce. "Twenty. In a cellar, that's this pole. In a street it's the substation, or the pump house, or the cell in somebody's cold-cupboard, and when it gets there it'll foul the tune and that cell will drift until somebody like me comes to quench it." He swung the pole away and racked it. "Twenty-four. Now the other thing."

He scooped a pinch from the edge of the heap onto a glass dish, carried it four paces off, and set it on the brick. He beckoned the second-year. She came, raised her fist beside the dish, and lit her blade for the space of three counts, a hard green-white line in the dim hall. Then it was dark again, and she stepped back.

Druce had not waited to watch. He was on his knees at the cloth with a flat steel pan in each hand, and Kiva forgot the dish.

She had spent her whole life watching one person work with her hands. She knew what it looked like when someone had done a thing ten thousand times, and it looked like this. He folded two corners of the cloth inward so that the heap ran together, and he took it up in the pans in four long passes, never lifting the steel higher than a hand above the canvas, and each panful went into a squat round tin that he had stood ready with its lid off. There were four tins. He pressed each lid until it clicked, wound a waxed cord twice around the rim, and pulled it through its own loop, and all the while he went on counting up the tube in that soft unhurried voice, as though he were telling a child how many stairs there were.

"Forty-one," he said, and set down the fourth tin. "That's the catch. Forty-one is slow. I'm an old man and I was talking."

He waited. Nobody on the benches spoke.

"Sixty. Settled." He picked up the glass dish and carried it to the foot of the window, and held it up under the lamps so that the front rows could see, and people in the back stood. "Look at it."

Kiva stood too. The pinch of ash on the dish was grey, like any ash. But there was a line in it, a crease as thin as a hair and as bright as a scratch on new tin, green-white, running straight across the middle.

"That's her blade," said Druce. "Three counts of it, at arm's length, and it will be her blade until somebody salts it. We call that printed. Put printed ash in a lamp and the lamp runs with an edge in its tune. Put a tin of it in a substation." His eyes came up through the glass and found the back row, and rested there no longer than they had rested anywhere else. "Some of you have seen what a cell does with a pattern it was never meant to hold."

He tipped the dish into a box of wet salt. Even through the glass, she heard it hiss.

Kiva sat down slowly. She was thinking about a heap of ash that had come apart all over her at the pump-room door, warm as bread, while two of her doors stood jammed open and the third burned to get out. The yield sheet for Team Seven had been pinned to the Cellhouse gate the following Monday, a single line under the rosters, and she had read it without understanding it. *T7, tin 4: salted, unread.*

She understood it now.

---

"Four jobs," said Druce, when he had come back up.

He chalked them on the slate beside the call order, one beneath another, and he pressed so hard that the chalk squeaked.

*KILL. HOLD. CARRY. CATCH.*

"Kill cuts the knot. Hold keeps the thing where you want it and keeps the next one off your backs. Carry brings out whoever's hurt, and somebody always is. On my floor it's a sandbag. In the field it's your friend. Catch tins the yield." He underlined the last word. "A team that does three of those has failed its response, and I don't much care which three. You'll declare the roles aloud to me before every floor, the same as you declared them in the Cellhouse, and if you change one, you say so first."

He turned from the slate.

"Three rules, and then I'll let you make a mess. From the cut to sixty is the count. Inside the count and inside three paces, nothing is lit. Not an Edge, not a ward, not a stance. We call that dark hands, and I will fail a floor for a lit thumbnail. Second. Inside the count, the catcher owns the floor. If your catcher says *dark*, you go dark, and I don't care if you're a Torch with a medal. Third." He looked at them over his grey fingertips. "Any one of you may stop a response at any time for any reason. The word is *off*. You say it three times, so that nobody mistakes it for conversation, and everyone goes dark and walks back to the line. An abort called early costs you nothing with me. An abort called late costs you a little. An abort nobody called, because the best blade on the team thought he had it, has cost this city more people than I can name."

Beside her, Kellan had gone very still.

"The mark is twenty," said Druce. "The book gives a Handful twenty-four drams. Tin twenty of them at Grade Second or better, with nothing printed and nothing fouled and nobody hurt, and you have a clean response. You'll practice here until I think you're fit to be seen. Then you'll do it once at a held tear, under a chartered officer, when the season gives us one. That's the first step. After it, the Compact will let you try for a Flask." He nearly smiled. "After it, you'll also be paid. A small share, in ash, on a team book. I mention it because I find it concentrates the mind."

He bent and lifted a canvas roll from under the table, and a wooden carrier with six squat tins in it, and two steel pans, and a brush.

"One kit to a team. Who's catching for Seven?"

Nobody answered. They had not got as far as roles. Kiva opened her mouth to say that she could not be anything yet, that there was a condition with the Chair's signature under it, and Druce's eyes dropped to her sling.

"Ah," he said. "Good. The one with the broken wing always learns the catch. It's the only job on the floor you can practice with a dead husk, and it doesn't want a door." He put the roll into her good arm. It was heavier than it looked. "No doors at all, in fact. I'm told that's something you've been working on."

---

The dead husk was a bucket.

Druce had a row of them along the back wall, filled with spent ash that had been quenched and dried and was as dead as sand, and he sent the teams down to the settling floor in threes and walked among them with a bucket on his hip. He did not say when he was going to tip it. He simply stopped somewhere, beside a tank or on a bridge or in the middle of somebody's argument, and poured a Handful of grey dust onto the brick and said, "One."

The first time he did it to Team Seven, Kiva had the cloth half unrolled and was still working out which way the slope ran.

"Two," said Druce. "Three."

She dropped to her knees. Her left knee told her at once what it thought of that. She pulled the cloth toward the heap with her right hand and found that a waxed canvas two paces square was a different thing with one arm than it had looked with two, because the far corners dragged and folded under. Anwen caught one and squared it without being asked.

"Is that allowed?" said Kiva.

"Hold may help the catcher," said Druce. "Ten. Dark, and inside the count, anybody may. Eleven."

The pans were worse. She could work one with her right hand, and she got the edge of it under the heap and drew it up, and the dust slid neatly off the far side because there was no second pan to stop it. She tried bracing the other against her boot. That was better. It was not good. By thirty she had most of the heap on steel and a grey fan of it spread across the brick where the cloth had rucked, and Perrin, who could not hold anything, was kneeling beside her with his bandaged hands in his lap, saying, "Left. More left. No, your left. That's a pan, Kiva, not a shovel."

"Thirty-eight," said Druce.

She got the first tin between her knees and filled it. She pressed the lid. Then she had to wind the cord, and there was no way to wind a cord around a tin with one hand, so she brought her left hand out of the sling as far as the fingers and pinched the waxed end between her first finger and her thumb.

She could not feel it.

She could see it. She could see her own finger and thumb closed on the cord. But the finger told her nothing at all, no bite of wax and no roughness, only a kind of thick dull pressure, as if she were holding the cord through the side of a boot. She wound it twice with her right hand and pulled it through the loop and did not know whether her left had kept the end tight or let it slide.

"Sixty," said Druce. "Settled. Ninety-four when you finished, which we won't discuss." He bent, picked up her tin, and turned it upside down.

The lid fell off. Six drams of dead grey dust poured onto the brick at her knees.

Nobody laughed. Three other teams were on the floor, and most of them were kneeling in grey fans of their own.

"That," said Druce mildly, "is a street lamp for a fortnight." He crouched in front of her, joints cracking, and looked at her left hand where it stuck out of the sling. "Which fingers?"

"The first two."

"Backwash?"

"Yes."

"Coming back?"

"Slowly. They said a week or two."

"Then stop asking them things they can't tell you." He took the empty tin and pressed the lid on with the heel of his hand, and it clicked, a small, flat, definite sound, like the click of a tram's brake. He pulled it off and did it again. "A good lid says so. Listen for it. Don't feel for it. Wind the cord with whichever hand works and hold the end under your thumb or in your teeth, I don't mind which. And when the tin's sealed, you say *seal check*, and somebody with a whole pair of hands puts a thumb on it before it goes in the carrier." He set the tin down. "The catchers who were too proud to ask for a seal check have salted more ash than every bad blade in Meridian. You're in luck. You've an excuse."

He stood and went away with his bucket, and a moment later she heard him say, somewhere behind her, "One."

"Again," said Kiva.

"He's on the other side of the room," said Perrin.

"I know. Pour the tin out. I'll do the seals until he comes back."

---

They came up the side stair at four, grey to the elbows, and sat on the Cellhouse steps in the sun because nobody had the strength to go anywhere else.

Kiva's best count had been seventy-one. She had sealed nine tins, and Anwen had put her thumb on each of them, and three had been loose.

"I have to tell you something," said Kellan. "All three of you. I'd rather you heard it from me."

He had not sat down. He was standing two steps below them with his hands in his pockets, looking not at them but at the plaza, where the recruiters' awnings had been taken down and a man with a cart was sweeping up the last of the anniversary ribbon.

"The first-team master sent for me this morning. First Division fighters can't enter a live lane without the first-step license. The live lanes open in three weeks. He's looked at the call order, and at who's on Team Seven and what the Chair wrote about her, and he says the board will approve a transfer for cause." He spoke evenly, a man reading a tag aloud. "Team Nineteen lost its Ember-door yesterday. A broken wrist, in a practice lane. They stand nineteenth. If another tear comes this month they'll be called in the second group, and I'd be licensed inside a fortnight. He needs my answer by Saturday noon."

Nobody said anything.

"So you're leaving," said Perrin at last. He said it lightly. It did not come out light.

"I haven't decided. I'm telling you where I'm standing while I decide. That's what a card is for." Kellan turned, and his dark steady eyes went to Kiva. "You can't go on a live floor until Dane signs. Do you know what she needs to see?"

"No."

"Does she?"

"I don't know that either," said Kiva. It was the truth, and saying it made her stomach turn over. "She hasn't said."

"Then I can't measure it." He took his hands out of his pockets. "Can I read your book?"

She had not expected that. The book went to Mara every night and to Orla every week, and to Ysra Crest when Ysra Crest asked. Nobody else had ever held it. She took it out of her coat anyway, the grey waxed card gone soft at the corners, and gave it to him.

He read it standing up. He read the Terrace Ring, and he was quiet for a moment over his own name. He read the Cellhouse. He read the page from the hallway on Coper Street, and she saw him read the last column twice.

*Why: Don't know. Writing it down so somebody can.*

He closed it and handed it back.

"Saturday," he said. "I'll tell you before I tell the master." He went down the steps.

"Saturday," said Anwen, when he had gone. She was looking at her list, at a line near the top that she had underlined twice. "Graywater posts on Saturday."

Perrin lay back on the warm stone with his wrapped hands on his chest. "Everything's Saturday," he said. "I don't see why I should be the only one of us with nothing to dread. I'm going to find something."

---

The shop was still open when she got home, and Mara was at the bench with the week's cell books.

The municipal van had come as usual, then. Kiva stood on the step a moment and looked through the glass at the stack under its brass weight, and at her aunt's bent head, and felt something in her chest let go that she had not known was clenched. The notice from the Works had not come on Monday. It had not come today. Mara had said the end of the week, and Mara was not often wrong about the Works.

Upstairs, the dead cell panel in the hallway had a tag on its handle in square capitals. *QUENCHED 13TH. NOT REFILLED PENDING REVIEW. M.R.* Kiva went past it with her elbow tucked in, though there was nothing left inside to hear her.

She had brought home six empty tins from the Kennel, signed for in Druce's book, and after supper she put the field book in its place beside her plate and lined the tins up on the kitchen table and began. Lid. Click. Cord, twice round, with the end pinned under her right thumb. Through the loop. She said "seal check" to the cold-cupboard and turned the tin over herself.

Mara washed the plates and did not ask. On the fourth tin she dried her hands and sat down across the table, and watched Kiva's left hand lying useless beside the carrier, with its two fingers a little straighter than the others.

"Your fingers are lying to you," she said.

"The warden said to stop asking them."

"He's right. But you've got eight more, and you're not asking those either." Mara reached over and took the tin. She did not press the lid with her palm, the way Druce had. She set the tip of her third finger on the rim, the ring finger of her left hand, and ran it once around, as lightly as she would have tested a hairspring. "There. It's proud on this side. A hair. You'd have lost a pinch out of that by morning." She pushed the lid home. "Use this one. It's the laziest finger you've got, so it hasn't any bad habits. It'll learn."

Kiva looked at her aunt's hands. Then she looked at them again.

Mara's left hand was holding the tin. Her right hand, the scarred one, lay flat on the table and had not moved.

"You used your left," said Kiva slowly. "You always use your left now. For anything fine." She had watched those hands at the bench every day of her life. She had seen the reading glasses go up and the tweezers come out ten thousand times, and for three years she had seen them come out in the wrong hand and had never once thought about it. "Mara. Can you feel with the right?"

Her aunt turned the scarred palm up under the lamp. The burn ran across it, pale and shiny, from the base of the first finger to the heel.

"Along the edges," she said. "Not in the middle. The middle went the day it happened and it never came back." She said it as she would have read out a fault note. "I taught the left to read instead. It took most of a year. It's as good now as the right ever was, and some days better, because it had to learn on purpose." She closed the hand. "I ought to have told you that as well. I seem to have a drawer full of things I ought to have told you."

"Does it—"

"No. It doesn't hurt. It just doesn't report." Mara set the tin back in the carrier, in its place in the row. "Yours are coming back. The medic said so, and I've had my thumb on them every night, and they are. That's not what I'm telling you. I'm telling you what to do in the meantime, and what to do if there's ever a time they don't." She nodded at the tins. "Again. With the lazy finger."

Kiva did it again.

It was strange, and slow, and the third finger of her left hand was clumsy and unwilling and wanted its neighbors to do the work. But on the sixth tin she felt it, the faintest ridge where the lid sat a hair proud of the rim, and she pressed it down and heard the click.

When all six stood sealed in the carrier, she opened the book.

*Exercise:* Catch, dead ash. Kennel floor, then kitchen.

*What went wrong:* One arm. Cloth rucked. Could not feel the cord. First tin opened when the warden turned it over.

*Who was hurt:* Nobody.

*What it cost:* 6 drams of dead ash on the floor. Would have been a street lamp for a fortnight.

*How long to come back:* Count 94 first go. Best 71. Mark is under 60.

*Second go:* Listen for the lid. Seal check, out loud, every tin. Third finger reads.

She stopped at the last column, with the pencil over it, and this time she found that she knew what belonged there.

*Why:* Mara reads with her left. She has done for three years. I never saw.

---

## CHAPTER — books/book-2680-opening/manuscript/chapter-10.md

# Chapter 10 — Kiva: The Tin

On Wednesday the warden gave the catchers slates and sent them up to the glass.

There were eleven of them, one from each team that had not yet been called, and they sat in a row along the front bench of the cast room with their kits at their feet. Below them, on the settling floor, the other three members of Team Nineteen were unrolling a cloth.

"You'll each write two things," said Druce. He stood behind the bench with his grey fingers folded. "Before the kill, where you think it will fall. After the fall, which way it runs. Don't tell me what you'd like. Tell me what the floor is going to do."

Kiva balanced the slate on her knee and studied the floor.

She had learned, in one afternoon on her knees, that it was not flat. Nothing in that hall was flat. The brick between the tanks had been laid with a fall toward each grate, so that two hundred years ago the refinery men could sluice it down, and a heap of dust that landed a pace from a tank's lip would creep toward the lip like water finding a drain. The plank bridges bounced. And along the north wall ran a shallow gutter, with four open grates in it, that she had not noticed at all until she had swept a pinch of dead ash into one.

Team Nineteen had laid their cloth in the middle of the widest stretch of brick, which was sensible. Their hand-door boy stood behind it with a ward half raised. Their still-door girl had the sandbag in a corner and was pretending it had a broken leg.

Their Ember-door was new. She was a wiry girl with cropped red hair and a Saltmere-green band on her sleeve, and she was bouncing on her toes at the far end of the floor, nowhere near the cloth.

"That's Selka Dray," said Perrin's voice, low, from the bench behind. The rest of Team Seven had been allowed up to watch, and he had put himself directly at Kiva's shoulder. "Second Division. She's standing in until Kellan makes up his mind. I fought her in the open lane. Three to two, to me." He paused. "She hits like a door. I've still got the mark."

Kiva was not watching Selka Dray's blade. She was watching her feet, and the long straight line between those feet and the steel door.

A husk went at the brightest thing. It went straight. If the brightest thing in that hall was going to be a lit blade at the south end, a dozen paces from the cloth, then the cloth might as well have been left in its roll.

She wrote on the slate. *South tank, east lip. Runs into the grate.*

Druce pulled the lever.

---

It took Selka Dray four seconds.

Kiva had to admit that they were a lovely four seconds. The husk came out of the door and found her at once, and she did not wait for it. She went to meet it in two Strides with the struts flashing on her calves, and her blade was on her fist and lit before the second landed, a quick hot yellow, and she cut the thing through the knot at a dead run and was three paces past it before it had finished falling.

It fell on the east lip of the south tank.

About half of it fell there. The other half went over the lip and down the sloping tank floor in a long soft slide, and then the heap on the lip began to creep after it, lifting and settling, and Team Nineteen's catcher came sprinting over the plank bridge with his cloth flapping behind him like a flag.

"One," said Druce, to the glass. "Two."

By the time the cloth was down there was nothing left on the lip to put it under. The catcher went into the tank on his knees with a pan in each hand. Selka Dray stood on the bridge above him, breathing easily, with her blade still lit and held low, ready for the next one.

Kiva saw it. She saw Druce see it. His mouth went thin, and he said nothing at all, and wrote something on a card.

"Sixty. Settled."

They tinned nine drams. Nearly all of the rest had gone through the grate.

Druce walked down the row of catchers and read the slates over their shoulders. He read hers and stopped.

"Why the grate?"

"The tank floors slope to them. Everything in there runs downhill." She hesitated. "And she was always going to kill it where she met it. She didn't look at the cloth once. So I looked at where she was standing, and where it would come from, and drew the line."

"Hm," said Druce. He moved on.

"*Hm,*" said Perrin in her ear. "You've had a *hm* from the warden. I've been here two days and he hasn't noticed I'm alive."

"You haven't got any hands."

"I've got a lot of other qualities."

Below, Team Nineteen had squared themselves for a second release. They had moved the cloth to the south tank's lip this time, which was at least where the last one had died, and the still-door girl had dragged her sandbag out of the way. Druce went to the tube. "Dray. Dark blade until it's on the canvas. Kill it where your catcher is." He put his hand on the lever.

The second husk came through the door, and Kiva frowned.

She could not have said what was wrong with it. It was the right size. It had the low front and the four thick limbs. But the light in that hall was poor and yellowish, and the front of the thing seemed to catch it. From its blunt nose back over what would have been its shoulders, there was a faint sheen on the grey, like the skin on a bowl of warm milk.

Selka Dray had not noticed, and she lit her blade. The husk swung to her and came, and she let it get onto the canvas this time, and stepped in, and cut.

Her blade skated.

It struck the husk's front quarter and slid off sideways with a high clear ringing noise. Kiva had heard that noise before. It was the sound Anwen's ward made when a clerk tapped it with a rod.

Dray staggered. She recovered faster than Kiva could have, and cut again, harder, and the blade rang again and turned. The husk drove into her hip. She went back two paces, swearing, with the hand-door boy shouting something, and cut a third time straight down with both fists on the hilt of nothing, and whatever was on the front of the thing broke.

It broke like a dropped plate. A thin bright curve of something stood in the air for half a breath, silver, the size and shape of a pot lid. Then it fell in pieces and the pieces were ash before they hit the brick, and her blade went on through into the knot, and the rest of the husk poured down after.

Nobody on the bench moved.

"One," said Druce. His voice was exactly as soft as it had been. "Catcher. That's yours. Two."

He counted the whole sixty. He watched the tins go into the carrier. Then he went to the tall desk at the end of the cast room, where a book as thick as a doorstep lay chained to the wall, and turned to the last written page and uncapped a pen.

Kiva was at the end of the bench. She could see his hand move, and she could see the line he wrote under it, because he wrote it large.

*Third this month.*

"Was that a bad batch?" said one of the other catchers, a Wren House boy with a scared face. "Warden? Is the Kennel making bad ones?"

"The Kennel doesn't make batches." Druce capped the pen. "It's one tear, held in one lattice, and it has given this school one husk at a time for sixty years, and every one of them is in this book. I've kept the book for twenty-two of those. Handfuls don't wear a skin. Flasks do, sometimes, out on a real line. I've seen it at Flask twice in my life." He looked down at the page. "I'd not seen it at a Handful until the fourth of this month."

"Then what is it?"

"It's the third one this month," said Druce. "When I know more than that, I'll write more than that. Slates, please."

---

Kellan was at the Old Yard gate on Thursday night before Orla had the padlock off.

He stood under the brass sign with his hands in his pockets and read it, all of it, including the part about sunset. Then he looked at the sky, which had been dark for an hour.

"It says ceremonial use only."

"Every time I teach something, it's a ceremony," said Orla. She pushed the gate open with her stick. "Ask anyone. Did somebody invite you, Mr. Renn?"

"No."

"Good. I don't hold with invitations. Mind the rut."

He minded it. He came down the lane behind Kiva and Perrin with the wary step of someone walking through another family's kitchen, and when Orla hung the lamp on its hook he stood at the edge of the light and did not sit. Anwen was already there, on the upturned crate beside Orla's, with her coat buttoned to the chin. She had come straight from the Wren House lanes, and there was a ward-burn on the heel of her hand that she had not mentioned.

"I said Thursday," she told Kiva. "I said nine."

"I've got one arm until tomorrow."

"I know. I came to hear the excuse in person."

"There's no excuse and there's no nine," said Orla, lowering herself onto the crate. "There's a medic's order that runs until morning, and I don't cross medics. But the order says she's not to use the arm. It says nothing at all about doors." She pointed the stick at Kiva. "Sling off. Hands at your sides. You're going to do the drill with nothing in them."

"Nothing?"

"No bar. No ward. No stance. Open the door, let it stand open, shut it. Next door. I want to know how long your doors take when you aren't hauling anything through them."

Kiva worked her arm out of the sling. It felt thin and strange, and the skin along the new length of the heat-lines pulled when she straightened her elbow. She stood in the middle of the lane with her hands hanging and found that she did not know how to begin.

She had never opened the Ember door without asking it for something. She tried it now. She thought about the slab on her forearm, warm and heavy, and about the place behind it where the asking came from, and she simply leaned on that place without asking.

It opened. She felt it stand there, awake, with nothing in its hands, looking about.

She breathed out, and it shut.

Then the hand door, cool and close and curious. Open. It reached at once for Anwen and found that Anwen had no ward up, and while it was still puzzled, Kiva closed it. Then the still door, heavy under her feet. Open. *Thank you.* Shut.

She stood in the dark with her chest cool and quiet and knew before anybody spoke that it had been quicker.

"Seven," said Orla.

"Seven?"

"Seven seconds. I counted it twice in my head because I didn't believe the first." Orla's face in the lamplight was thoughtful. "Do you understand what that is? Your best with shapes is nine and a half. Two and a half seconds of that is not doors. It's furniture. You've been carrying an iron bar and a tea tray through three doorways and wondering why you kept catching the frame."

"That's not nine," said Anwen.

"No. It's seven."

"It's seven with nothing in her hands. I asked for nine with all of it." Anwen folded her arms. "I'm not being difficult. I want to see the thing I asked to see, and I don't want anyone telling her that was it."

"Nobody's telling her that was it," said Orla mildly. "Next week you'll get your nine. Tonight she's learned where the other two seconds live."

Kellan had come a step into the light. He was looking at Kiva's right hand.

"You watch it," he said.

Kiva turned. "What?"

"The metal. When it runs home. I saw you do it in the Ring and again on the mezzanine. You watch it all the way back to the cuff. You hold its hand." He raised his own right fist, and his long dark blade came off the steel and stood there, and then it was gone again, a smooth quick flow like a fish turning, and only after it was gone did the small breath leave his nose. "It knows the way. It's been going home to that slab for four years. If you escort it, it goes at the speed you can watch. If you let it go, it goes at its own." He lowered his hand. "That's half a second, at least. Probably more."

It was the longest thing he had ever said to her that did not have a number in it.

"Why are you telling me?" said Kiva.

He took the question seriously, which she was beginning to learn he did with every question.

"If I stay on Seven, I need you faster," he said. "If I go, it's cost me nothing. Either way I'd be stupid to keep it." He put his hands back in his pockets. "And it annoyed me. Watching you do it wrong."

Perrin made a sound like a kettle. "He's *helping*," he said. "Kiva, he's helping you, and he's found a way to make it sound like a complaint. I've never admired anyone more."

---

"Now," said Orla. "The other thing."

She reached into the canvas bag and took out one of Druce's squat tins, with its lid off, and after it a small flat case of worn leather. Inside the case was a brass balance no longer than her hand, with two pans on fine chains and a row of tiny weights in a slotted block.

"Home Line field scale," she said. "You weigh a yield on the bank before you trust anyone's word for it, including your own. Miss Pryce, would you fill that tin to six drams from the bag at your feet? It's dead ash. The warden lent it to me, and he counted it out, so don't spill."

Anwen filled it. She did it the way she did everything, in careful pinches, and the balance settled level.

Orla held the open tin out to Kiva.

"Both hands. Your left may hold a tin tonight and do nothing else. Hold it in front of your breastbone, level, as if it were a Handful somebody had bled for." She waited until Kiva had it. The dust lay a finger's width below the rim, grey and fine. "You told Ysra Crest that you don't know how to keep your doors out when you're frightened. I've thought about that sentence every day since. It's the truest thing in your book, and I can't sign anything until it changes. So we're going to frighten you."

"With what?"

"With Mr. Renn," said Orla. "Since he's here, and uninvited, and by a long way the best blade in this yard. Dark edge, flat of the blade, body and legs. He's going to hit you. You may not block. You may not step off your ground. You may not open a door, any door, for any reason. You may turn your body to keep the tin level, and that's all you may do." She took a stub of pencil out of her coat. "Afterward we weigh what's left. That's your number. Mr. Renn likes a number."

Kellan had not moved.

"She won't be defending," he said.

"That's the drill."

"I've never hit anyone who wasn't defending."

"Then you'll both learn something," said Orla. "Hit her properly or go home. I won't have her practicing against kindness. It was kindness I tried last time."

Kiva did not know what last time meant, and then, a moment later, looking at Orla's face, she did.

Kellan came and stood in front of her. The blade was on his fist, dark. Close to, it was a very long piece of metal.

"Ribs," he said quietly, "the first time. So you know."

"Don't tell her," said Orla.

He hit her in the ribs.

It was not hard. It was the flat of a blunt blade, swung at half his strength, and it would leave a mark and nothing worse. But she saw it come, and every part of her that had fought him in the Terrace Ring saw it come too, and the Ember door did not wait to be asked. It came open like a dog through a gate. Her right hand left the tin. The bar was half formed on her knuckles before the blow landed, and the bottom heat-line was hot and the second was warming, and the tin, left to one weak hand, tipped.

Dead ash went down her front in a soft grey spill.

"One," she said, much too late, and shut the door on a bar that had nowhere to go. Her face burned.

Orla held out her hand for the tin. She set it on the balance and moved the little weights with one fingernail, unhurried.

"Three drams and a tenth left. Two and nine-tenths on the ground." She wrote it down. "Two lines lit. Again. Miss Pryce, fill it."

The second time Kellan did not tell her. He looked at her ribs and hit her on the thigh.

That was worse, because she had braced for the ribs, and something in her had been so busy not flinching one way that it flinched the other. *Three and four-tenths on the ground.* The third time was worse again, and it was not Kellan at all, because Perrin said, "Can I have a go? I can't make a fist, but I've got shoulders," and before anybody had answered, he had walked into her like a tram.

The tin left her hands entirely. It landed in a rut four paces down the lane.

"That one doesn't count," said Kiva, from the ground.

"Everything counts," said Orla, writing. "Six drams lost. No lines lit, which is interesting. You were too surprised to be frightened. Mr. Cade, sit down."

"I helped," said Perrin. "I think I really helped."

Kiva got up. Her ribs hurt and her thigh hurt, and her left hand was shaking, and she was angry, with a mean hot useless anger that had nowhere to go, because the only person there to be angry at was a boy who was doing exactly what he had been asked. She took the filled tin from Anwen and held it at her breastbone and glared at the long dark blade.

It moved. She saw his shoulder turn before his arm did.

"Left ribs," she said.

She had not meant to say anything. It came out of her the way a reading came out of her at the bench, flat and automatic, the number called because the needle was there to be called. And the blow landed on her left ribs, exactly where she had said, and it hurt.

The Ember door lifted. She felt it come up to the threshold, eager, with its hackles raised.

It stayed there.

The bottom line was warm. The second was cool. Her hands were on the tin, both of them, and the grey surface of the ash had rocked to the rim and rocked back.

Orla weighed it for a long time.

"One and a tenth lost," she said. "One line." She looked up. "What did you do?"

"I don't know."

"You said something."

"I said where it was going." Kiva was still staring at the tin. "I don't know why that helped. I didn't plan it."

"Write it down anyway. Put *don't know* in the last column, if that's what you've got." Orla closed the balance case with a click. "That's enough for a first night. You'll be black and blue, and Mr. Renn looks as though he'd like to go and wash his hands."

Kellan did look like that. He had let the blade go the moment she called a halt, and he was standing with his empty fist held slightly away from his body.

"Same time tomorrow?" he said, not to Kiva but to Orla.

"I thought you hadn't decided if you were staying."

"I haven't." He frowned, as though the two facts did not fit together and he had only just noticed. "I'd still like to see the number go down."

---

When they had gone, Orla Dane sat on her crate in the dark and did her accounts.

She did not mean money. She took out her own field book, older and fatter than the three she had ruled for the children, and turned to the page where she had written the four weights, and looked at them by the lamp. Two and nine-tenths. Three and four. Six. One and one.

It was a real number. That was the thing she kept returning to. She had taught for twenty-one years on patience and field sense and the feeling in her chest when a student was coming right, and she had never once in that time owned a number for the only question that mattered, which was what a person did in the half second after they were surprised.

She had never surprised Jessamy Hart. She made herself look at that squarely, because the dark was a good place for it. She had been proud of how gently she brought him on. She had built him up a step at a time, with every step explained beforehand and every drill agreed, and he had been splendid, and she had put her name beside his on the strength of two years in which nothing had ever come at him sideways. The first thing that did was a live cell in front of four thousand people.

She had not needed to be crueler to him. She had needed to know.

Orla turned to a clean page. Her knee was aching in the deep, sour way that meant rain before morning, and she shifted it and wrote in the square, careful hand she kept for case files.

*What I would need to see, to sign for recovery and nothing else.*

*One. The tin. Five nights running, under half a dram lost, no second line. Not five good nights out of seven. Five running.*

*Two. Six seals of six, sound, with the bad hand, called and checked.*

She stopped there with the pencil lifted.

The third was the one she had been walking around since Saturday. A dark blade swung by a friend in a yard was not a husk. The girl knew Kellan Renn was not going to kill her. Some part of the body always knew. Orla could weigh tins until midsummer, and all she would have proved was that Kiva Rowan could keep her doors shut when she was hurt and embarrassed and angry, which was worth knowing, and was not the same as afraid.

There was a rule for it. It was older than the Compact, and it sat near the back of the license code, where nobody under fifty had any reason to look. A sponsor might walk an uncertified candidate onto a contained floor, once, under the sponsor's own Edge, for the purpose of deciding whether to sign. The sponsor was the declared Kill. The floor went in the Kennel book. If anything went wrong on it, the incident was the sponsor's.

She had not stood on a floor with a live thing in six years. She had not Stridden in seven. She had a stick.

*Three,* she wrote. *One floor under my own hand.*

She looked at it. It did not look any less foolish written down.

And what frightened her was not the contract, or the standing they would take away again, or even Ysra's long plain face across a desk. She had gone looking for that fear tonight and found the place where it used to live quite empty. What frightened her was a picture, very clear, of a knee folding at the wrong moment with a seventeen-year-old kneeling two paces away, trusting her to be between.

That was a fair thing to be frightened of. She wrote nothing about it. She would do the exercises the Wren House had given her, the ones she had been skipping since the autumn, and she would see what the knee said in a week.

*No hurry,* she wrote instead, at the bottom of the page, and underlined it. She had taught young Brannock for two years and never cured him of hurrying. She did not intend to leave any of her own in this.

Then she took down the lamp and went to walk the lane, alone, because her feet ought to remember it too.

---

The notice came on Friday by the noon post, in a long envelope with a red stripe.

Kiva was in the shop when the postman pushed it through the door. She had come home at midday because the sling was to come off, and Mara had said she would rather be the one to do it. She saw the stripe from across the room. So did Mara, who put down a pressure gauge, wiped her hands on her apron, and opened the envelope with a bench knife in one clean stroke.

She read it standing. Her face did not change, which told Kiva nothing, because Mara's face would not have changed if the letter had been on fire.

"Well," said Mara. She held it out. "Read it yourself. I'm not having any more drawers."

*MUNICIPAL WORKS LICENSING OFFICE. NOTICE OF REVIEW.*

*Grade Two Tuning License, M. Rowan, Coper Street. Following the household incident filing of the 13th (shared core, trip 02:44), the above license is placed under review. Pending hearing:*

*1. The holder shall not tune, refill, or seal any licensed cell.*

*2. Instrument repair and ledger reconciliation under the municipal contract may continue at the discretion of the Works.*

*3. The holder shall present all shop books to Examiner Rusk on the 9th of next month.*

*4. The holder shall state in writing whether the household condition of 2677 persists. If a minor of the household holds a Compact roster place, the holder shall furnish the accountable sponsor's certification status.*

Kiva read it twice, as she read everything from an office.

"It's not suspended," she said. "Not all of it."

"No. They've cut it in half and left me the half that's all paper." Mara took the letter back and looked at the first condition for a moment, and her left thumb moved across her right palm. "Tuning was a third of the money. A bit more in a bad winter. That regulator from Tallis Street, I can't touch that sort of thing now. They'll send it to somebody on the east bank who'll charge them double and miss the hurry." She folded the page along its creases. "But the van still comes. The books still come. I can mend a meter and tell the city when its numbers don't agree. It's half a loaf. I've eaten worse."

"Until the ninth."

"Until the ninth. Then a man called Rusk goes through eleven years of my handwriting, and I find out if I get the other half back." She set the letter under the brass weight, on top of the week's ledgers, where it could not be mistaken for a drawer. "Did you read the fourth one?"

"They want to know if Orla's signed."

"They want to know if anyone who knows what they're about has said you're safe to stand near things. It's a fair question. It's the same one I asked." Mara came around the bench. "Turn round. Let me have that knot."

Kiva turned, and felt her aunt's fingers at the back of her neck, working the medic's knot loose. The sling came away. Her arm hung at her side, lighter than she remembered and oddly cold, and when she bent the elbow, the long ache of the heat-lines woke and stretched like something that had been asleep in a cramped place.

Mara undid her collar button and looked. Kiva looked too, in the little spotted mirror over the shop sink.

The blisters had gone, and the skin had peeled where they had been and come in pink and tight. The lines themselves had faded from welts to three raised threads, darker than before. They still ran over her collarbone and past the point of her shoulder and halfway down the outside of her arm. She had half believed they would draw back as they healed, to where they had lain for four years.

They had not drawn back at all. The new length had stayed, like the mark a flood leaves on a wall.

"Tender?" said Mara.

"Only when I stretch."

"Then stretch. Twice a day. Scars that aren't stretched pull tight." Mara did the button up again, and her hand rested a moment on Kiva's shoulder. "You'll tell Orla about that fourth condition. Tonight, or whenever you see her. She ought to know it's there."

"I wasn't going to ask her to—"

"I know you weren't. I'm saying tell her. She's carrying my half loaf whether she likes it or not, and she's a right to know the weight." Mara's voice was level and dry. "And when you've told her, you tell her this as well, from me, in these words. If she signs one single day early on my account, I'll know. And so will the cell. She taught Brannock. She'll understand."

Kiva flexed her left hand. The first two fingers closed with the others, a little behind them, like the slow pair in a team of horses.

"I'll tell her," she said.

---

Anwen Pryce was at the Graywater hall by a quarter to nine on Saturday, and she was not the first.

There were eleven of them on the pavement of the Brickfields road, under the long slate-blue front of the company hall, and nobody spoke. They were all field-track candidates. She knew every face from the Wren House lanes. She knew to a tenth what each of them held on a ward, because she had written it down.

At nine exactly a clerk came out with a single sheet and pinned it in the glass case beside the door.

Anwen did not push. She waited until the two tall boys in front of her had read it and turned away, one of them grinning like an idiot and the other with a face like a shut door, and then she stepped up and read it from the top, although the whole of her wanted to read it from the bottom.

*GRAYWATER COMPANY. FIELD PLACEMENTS, WARD LINE, SEASON OF 2680.*

There were four names.

Hers was the fourth.

She read it three times to be sure of the spelling. *Pryce, A. Wren House.* And after it, where the other three names had nothing, a small black dagger, and at the foot of the sheet beside a second dagger, a line of small type.

*Conditional. First-step field license to be held within thirty days of posting.*

She stood very still on the pavement and let it go through her. It was like setting down something heavy that she had carried so long she had forgotten its weight. For about the space of four breaths she was simply and entirely happy, and she let herself be, because she had earned four breaths.

Then she took out her list and wrote *30 days = the 20th of next month* on it, and went in to find Doran Mace.

He was in a small office behind the muster room, with his ledger open and a cup of something that had gone cold. He stood to shake her hand, which nobody had done at the Wren House in two years.

"The other three tinned their Handful at the Ferren culverts on Monday," he said, when she had sat. "They were on teams near the top of the call. You're on the team at the bottom of it. I know why, and I was in the gallery, and I don't hold it against you. But the ward line doesn't put anyone on a culvert at night who hasn't caught ash with a live thing in the room. That isn't my rule. It's older than I am, and I've seen what it's for."

"I understand."

"Your team has a member who can't be certified yet." He said it without weight. "I asked. So here's what I can do. Team Eleven's hand-door has gone home to Saltmere with her mother ill. They stand eleventh on the call, and they're licensed except for her seat, and the board lets a field officer move a placed candidate for cause. The warden will put Eleven back on the end of a license day to seat a new hand-door. The second group's day is full, so it would be the third group's, twenty-seven to forty, whenever the season gives us that tear. One word from you, and you walk in with Eleven that day and walk out licensed."

Anwen looked at her list. She did not need to. It gave her hands something to do.

It was a good offer. It was exactly the offer she would have written for herself if somebody had handed her the pen. It asked nothing of her except that she go where she was going anyway, a little faster and by a shorter road.

And she thought of Kiva on her knees on the settling floor, grey to the elbow, saying *again* to an empty tin.

"How long does it stand?" she said.

"Until that bell rings. The third group's. After that Eleven goes in with a Wren House reserve."

"That's the same bell that calls Seven." She worked it through once, to be certain. "Then I'd like you to hold the seat until it rings. Not after. If Team Seven's whole when it rings, with all four certified, I go in with Seven. If it isn't, I go in with Eleven, that morning, and I don't look back at it."

Doran Mace leaned back in his chair. "That's having it both ways, Miss Pryce."

"Yes," said Anwen. "I'd like it said that I know that." She met his eyes. "I'm not giving up a placement to be loyal. I've wanted this since I was eleven and I've got thirty days. But I've been through one run with those three and a week on the Kennel floor, and I know what they'll tell me when something goes wrong, which is the truth, straight away, out loud. I don't know that about Eleven. I'd be walking onto a live floor with three people I've never heard say *off*." She folded the list. "So I'd rather have Seven, if I can have it. And if I can't, I'll take the other. That's the order. I've checked it."

He studied her for a moment longer, and then he laughed, not unkindly, and wrote a line in the ledger.

"Held until the bell," he said. "You'll tell her?"

"This morning. To her face."

"She won't thank you."

"She will, actually," said Anwen, and stood up. "That's why I'd rather have Seven."

---

She told her on the Cellhouse steps at noon, with the sun on the stone and Perrin eating a meat bun very carefully out of the tips of his fingers. The bandages had come off his hands that morning. Underneath, the palms had gone from purple to a blotched and startling yellow-green.

Kiva listened to all of it. Fourth of four. The dagger. Thirty days. Team Eleven, and the bell.

"So if the bell goes before Orla's signed," said Kiva, "you go."

"I go. That morning."

"And if you go, Seven's three, and one of the three can't set foot on the floor." Kiva said it to see how it sounded. It sounded like a sum that came out right. "So Seven doesn't run. And Perrin and Kellan wait for the tear after that."

"Yes."

"All right," said Kiva.

Anwen waited. "That's all?"

"You've got a placement." Kiva found, somewhat to her own surprise, that she was smiling, and that the smile was real, and that it had got in ahead of everything else. "Anwen. You've got a *Graywater placement.* You've had a poster of Rhea Sorn on your door since you were eleven. I'm not going to sit here and be sorry for myself about the footnote."

Anwen's level face did something it very seldom did, which was to come apart at one corner.

"I wanted it said," she managed. "The bell part."

"It's said. I'd have been angry if you'd hidden it." Kiva bumped her with her shoulder, the way Perrin did, though more gently. "Fourth of four. They took four this year."

"They took four," said Anwen. "I counted."

Kellan came up the steps while Perrin was still trying to shake Anwen's hand without using his palms. He stopped two steps below them, where he had stood on Tuesday.

"I went to see the master," he said. "I told him I'm filing nothing. I stay on Seven."

Nobody had expected it put quite so flatly. Perrin lowered his bun.

"Why?" said Kiva.

"Two reasons. I'll give you both, and you can check them." Kellan held up a finger. "I asked the warden for Nineteen's Kennel book. Three floors this week. Three kills in under six seconds, and they tinned nine drams, eleven, and seven. Nobody on that team looks at the cloth. They'd fail a field Handful with me exactly as they'd fail it without me, only quicker." A second finger. "And it would be a ruling. The master would ask, and the board would agree, and I'd go up the call twenty-one places because someone in an office decided I was worth it. I've said for a year what I think of places that are rulings." He did not look at Kiva when he said it, and then, deliberately, he did. "I can't say it about yours and then take one."

Kiva did not know what to say to that. Nobody had ever chosen a worse place in a queue as a matter of arithmetic and offered her the working.

"And the quench," Kellan added, more quietly. "The board has my name on a failed run. I'd like a second go at the same thing with the same people. That's what the column's for."

"So Anwen's got one foot out of the door," said Perrin slowly, "and Kellan's got both feet in, on principle, and I've got hands like a pair of old cabbages." He looked from one to the other. "I notice that nobody has offered *me* a transfer. I want it known that I'd have turned it down. I'd have been very noble about it."

"Nobody wants you, Perrin," said Anwen.

"Stonehand wants me. Stonehand gave me a bursary. I'm only saying a person likes to be asked."

Kiva laughed with the rest of them. But underneath the laughing she was doing the sum again, and it kept coming out the same. One of them was staying because of what he had read in her book. One was staying unless a bell rang. One had never been given the choice. And the whole of it rested on a signature that belonged to someone else, which she could not earn any faster by wanting it, and would not ask for.

She would tell Orla tonight where everybody stood. Orla had a right to know the weight.

She would not ask her to hurry. Mara had been quite clear about what a cell did with that.

---

## CHAPTER — books/book-2680-opening/manuscript/chapter-11.md

# Chapter 11 — Kiva: Under Her Hand

The litter weighed seventy kilos, and on Monday morning Kiva carried one end of it up and down the Low Lane for four minutes while Noa Bexley timed her.

It was the same sandbag in a coverall that Anwen had dragged out of the pump room. Every school in the Compact had a dozen of them. This one was lashed to a canvas stretcher between two ash poles, and its name, according to the faded stencil on its chest, was *PROPERTY OF C.L.S. 3RD — DO NOT REMOVE*, which Pru Finch had shortened long ago to Donny.

"Donny's been carried over the Stonehand line eleven times," said Pru, who had the other end. She was red in the face and talking in bursts. "And over ours, twice. In three years. Jude counted. One of the twice, Stonehand dropped him and he rolled."

"Less talking," said Jude from the wall. "More not dropping him."

Kiva was on the still door, and nothing else.

She had told the lane warden so at the gate, as the tag on the mast required, and he had written it in his book with the air of a man recording an outbreak. Then she had taken the front poles and let the weight come down into her feet, and found that the old trick worked as well on a sloping brick floor as it did on a tram. The litter did not get lighter. It simply stopped being hers to hold up. The ground held it, and she stood in between.

Walking was another matter. Stance wanted her planted, and every step meant giving a little weight back and taking it again, so that she went down the lane like somebody wading. Her left knee disliked it. But Pru's arms were trembling by the second length, and Kiva's were not.

"Four minutes," said Noa. "Down. Carefully. Don't look at the mast, either of you, I've only got the one pen."

They set the litter on the bricks. Pru sat on Donny's legs and blew her hair out of her eyes.

Noa was already at the mast's little glass door with a rule and a pencil. The Low Lane's mast was sixty years old and drew a single line for whoever happened to be lit in front of it, so he had made everybody else in the room go dark and stand against the wall while she carried, and he had made Pru do her share on plain muscle, which was why she was the color of a beetroot. He measured the line twice. He wrote. He measured it again.

"Nine hundredths," he said.

"Of a dram?" said Jude.

"Of a dram. In four minutes, under load, walking." Noa turned round. The strip of tape wound about his left hand had three new pencil marks on it. "The book figure for a still-door carrying that weight for that long is about a tenth. A good Stonehand third-year would do it in eight or nine hundredths. So she's standard. A hair better than standard." He looked at Kiva as if she had been hiding it from him on purpose. "Your draw on the still door, alone, is somewhere near nine-tenths. It isn't three point one. It isn't anything like three point one."

"The bench says three point one."

"The bench puts you in front of a lamp-cell and wakes all three of you up, and then measures the argument." He tapped the tape. "This is one of you, with the other two shut out. I told you they couldn't both be right." He caught himself. "I mean it's one reading. On an old mast. I'd want twenty before I said it to anybody who mattered."

"You've said it to us," said Jude.

"You don't matter. Sorry. You know what I mean."

Kiva sat down on the end of the litter, because her knee had begun to insist. *A hair better than standard.* In four years nobody had ever put her name and that word in the same sentence. She did not trust it. One reading on an old mast was exactly the sort of thing Mara would have tagged *check* and set aside. But she noticed that she wanted very badly for there to be nineteen more.

"So can she carry him or not?" said Jude patiently.

"She can carry him all day," said Noa. "I don't think she knows that yet."

---

The school bell rang at ten, three strokes and then three more, and everyone in the Low Lane stopped.

"That's a call," said Pru. "That's a tear. Oh, I hate that bell. Whenever I hear that bell I think, somebody's going to come back with a face like paper and no eyebrows—"

Jude had already gone to the door. A runner was crossing the yard with a slate held over her head, shouting the same words at every range she passed. He listened and came back.

"River flats. Below the Weighbridge. Home Line's holding it. Second group's called for the license, thirteen through twenty-six, muster by noon." He looked at Kiva. "Not yours."

It was not hers. Hers would be the tear after this one, whenever the season chose to send it. She thought of Kellan, who could have been walking to the river at this moment with a kit bag over his shoulder, and of Selka Dray, who was walking there in his place.

"That's two tears in nine days," said Noa slowly. He was unwinding tape. "Ferren on the fourteenth. This. How many do we usually get in a spring? In the whole district?"

"Four," said Jude. "Five in a wet year."

"And it isn't the end of the month." Noa wrote something very small on the tape and wound it up again. "Somebody ought to be counting that."

"Somebody is," said Jude. "You. Now pick up the other end of Donny. There's a sheet gone up on the range door, and our first meet's on it. Saturday week. The fourth." He rubbed the chipped tooth with his thumb, a thing he did, Kiva had noticed, when he was looking at something he could not fix. "Stonehand, at Stonehand. Third Division lane's first on the card, so the whole gallery will be fresh."

---

They drilled the tin every night that week, Sundays too.

Orla had sent word round on Friday that the Old Yard would be open at eight until further notice. Kiva had gone early that night to tell her about the fourth condition, and about Anwen's bell, and about Kellan staying. She had laid it all out in order, as flatly as she could, and then given her Mara's message in Mara's words.

Orla had listened to the end. Then she had snorted through her crooked nose.

"Tell your aunt I heard her," she said. "And tell her I'd already written it down." And she would say no more about it.

So Kiva stood in the lane each night with six drams of dead ash at her breastbone, and somebody hit her.

Kellan came every evening and did not explain it. Perrin came when his Hall let him out, and now that his palms could bear it he had begun to use them, flat and heavy, a Stonehand shove that arrived all at once like a door blowing shut. Between the two of them she never knew which it would be. That was the point, Orla said. A husk did not send a card.

Friday's weights were eight-tenths and six. Too much. On Saturday, with the news from the Cellhouse steps still warm in her, she lost four-tenths and lit one line, and Orla drew a small square in the margin of her book beside the number and said nothing. Sunday there was another square. On Monday, with her arms still humming from the litter, there were two blows and two weights and neither was over three-tenths.

By Wednesday there were four squares in Orla's margin, and Kiva had worked out why.

She had been turning it over since the first night, in the bath and on the tram and at the kitchen table with her pencil stopped above the last column. *Calling it helped. Don't know why.* She could do it now nearly every time. The shoulder moved, and her mouth said *right thigh* or *ribs* or simply *now*, and the blow came, and the Ember door reared up to the threshold and found the doorway already full of a word.

She understood it on the Tuesday, going home across the river, watching the needle on the tram's meter.

In the Cellhouse she had called every tenth. *Six and three. Six and seven, seventy seconds.
