Seven, fifty seconds.* She had stood with her hand on a cell that was running away, with husks on the stair below her, and she had not been afraid at all, or she had been and it had not mattered, because she had a needle to read and a number to send down to Anwen every few breaths.

And then she had pressed the hurry out, and the needle had stopped at seven and three, and she had stopped calling.

There had been nothing left to call. She had stood there for twenty seconds with her hand door open and her chest full of joy, and no number in her mouth at all. And then the husk had come up the stair.

It was not the fear that had opened the doors. She said it to Orla on Wednesday night, before the first blow, because she wanted somebody to check it. "It's the not counting. When I'm calling something, the calling's in the doorway. When I stop, the door's just standing there, and whatever I'm feeling walks straight through. Frightened or pleased. It doesn't care which."

Orla had the brass balance half out of its case. She put it down.

"Say the Cellhouse again," she said. "The last number you called before you went to the tune."

"Seven. Fifty seconds."

"She's right," said Kellan, from the edge of the light. He had his eyes shut, the way he did when he was reading something off the inside of his own head. "Seven, fifty seconds. Then *changing role*. Then *it's holding*, and after that nothing with a number in it until she was on the floor shouting at me." He opened his eyes. "Twenty-eight seconds without a call. I thought at the time you were concentrating."

"I was being happy," said Kiva. "It turns out that's worse."

"Huh," said Kellan.

Orla looked at her for a long moment in the yellow light. "There's a thing they say on the Line," she said. "I never liked it, because it sounded like something off a tea towel. *Give your fear something to carry.*" She picked the balance up again. "I think you've just told me what it means, and it's taken you a fortnight, and I had twenty years. Tin."

That night Kiva lost nothing at all, twice running. The dead ash rocked to the rim under Perrin's shove and rocked back, and when Orla set the tin on the scale the little brass pans hung dead level.

"Five," said Orla, and drew the square.

"Five what?" said Perrin.

"Never you mind. Miss Pryce has been sitting on that crate for an hour with her arms folded. Kiva. Give the young woman her nine, with the furniture, and for pity's sake don't watch the metal."

It was the hardest part, not watching. The bar ran off her fist and she wanted to see it home, every inch, the way she would have followed a part back into its housing. She looked at the crooked post instead, black at the far end of the lane. She felt the copper take the weight. She breathed out. Ward, curved, folded. Stance, and thank you.

"Eight and eight-tenths," said Orla.

Nobody spoke. Kiva put her hand to her chest, where the lines lay cool.

"That's nine," said Anwen. She unfolded her arms. "That's under nine. I want it said that I saw it." And then, because she was Anwen, she took out her list and wrote it down.

---

Orla kept her back when the others had gone to the gate.

"Tomorrow at three," she said. "The Kennel. You and I are going on the settling floor together, with a live Handful, and I am going to kill it, and you are going to tin it."

Kiva thought she had misheard.

"I'm not certified."

"No. That's why." Orla leaned on her stick. "There's a rule at the back of the license code. A sponsor may take an uncertified candidate onto a contained floor, once, under the sponsor's own Edge, to decide whether to sign. I'm the Kill. Nobody else is on the floor. It goes in the Kennel book, and if it goes wrong, the incident is mine and not yours. I've written to the Chair to say I'm doing it. She wrote back one word." Her mouth twitched. "*Noted.*"

"Yours," said Kiva. "Your standing. Your contract."

"That's what a sponsor is. I did read the form before I signed it."

"On that knee?"

She had not meant it to come out so baldly. Orla did not seem to mind. She looked down at the knee herself, as a person looks at a dog of uncertain temper.

"On this knee," she agreed. "I've been doing what the healers told me to do with it since Friday, which is more than I've done since the autumn. It'll take a floor. It won't take two, and it won't Stride, so I'll be killing the thing standing still, the way my grandmother would have." She raised her eyes. "I've weighed tins for seven nights. They're good tins. But you've known every night that the boy with the blade wasn't going to hurt you, not really, and so has the part of you that opens doors. Tomorrow that part won't know anything of the kind. I need to see what you do when it doesn't."

"And if I open something?"

"Then I'll have a printed Handful and a truthful answer, and we'll go back to the yard." Orla's voice was quite calm. "I'm not hurrying you onto that floor, Kiva. I want that understood. Your aunt's hearing and Anwen's bell and that boy's place in the queue are all real, and none of them is my reason. I'm taking you tomorrow because five squares was the number I wrote down before any of that reached me, and you've filled it."

Kiva believed her. That was the frightening part. If Orla had been hurrying, there would have been something to push against.

"Catcher sets the mark," said Orla, turning toward the gate. "Druce will let you on the floor at half past two with a bucket. Find out where you want it killed."

---

She found the place with a pinch of dead ash.

It took her most of the half hour. She went along the brick between the middle tank and the south one on her hands and knees, dropping a little grey dust every pace and watching which way it crept, and the warden watched her from the foot of the stair and offered no opinion. Near the tanks, it slid toward the lips. Near the north gutter, it slid toward the grates. But there was one strip of floor, about three paces from the south tank's west corner, where the two falls met and cancelled, and a pinch dropped there lay where it landed like a cat in the sun.

She laid the cloth on it. She squared the weights. She set four tins in a row with their lids off, and the pans to hand, and the carrier behind her, and then she knelt at the south edge of the canvas and discovered that her mouth had gone dry.

Above the floor, behind the green glass, three faces were watching. Anwen was upright and still. Perrin had both hands pressed flat to the window. Kellan stood a little apart, and she knew without being able to see it that he had a pencil.

Orla came across the plank bridge at three exactly.

She had left her coat somewhere. Her sleeves were rolled, and the old steel cuff was bare on her left forearm with the dark slab on it. She walked with the stick as far as the cloth, looked at where it lay, looked at the tanks and the gutter and back at the cloth, and nodded once. Then she stooped, stiffly, and laid the stick on the brick behind her, and straightened without it.

"Roles," said Druce, from the lever.

"Dane. Kill," said Orla. "Sponsor's floor."

"Rowan. Catch." Her voice came out thin. She tried again. "Door declared: none. Dark hands."

"Entered," said Druce. He wrote it in the chained book, which he had carried down with him and propped on a ledge. "One Handful, when you're ready, Lecturer."

Orla had placed herself on the far side of the cloth from the steel door, exactly where the second-year had stood, so that anything coming for her would have to cross the canvas to arrive. She was two paces from Kiva. She raised her right fist.

The metal came off the old cuff without haste and closed into the short, broad, leaf-shaped blade.

Then she lit it.

Kiva had known Orla Dane for four years and had never seen her edge-light. It was not white, like Kellan's, and it did not shiver, like her own. It was a deep, low yellow, the color of lamp oil, and it lay along the curve of the leaf as steadily as a pilot flame.

"Whenever you like, Warden," said Orla.

Druce pulled the lever.

---

It came through the inner door already turning.

There was no skin on this one. It was an ordinary husk, grey and low and blind, and it found the yellow blade in the time it took Kiva to draw one breath, and came for it.

It came straight. She had known that it would. She had drawn the line herself on a slate. What she had not known, sitting safely up behind the glass, was what the line looked like from the floor, on her knees, when it ran a single pace from her right shoulder.

She had no blade. She had a steel pan in each hand, like a woman about to serve a pudding. There was nothing whatever between her and it, and it was the size of a mastiff and coming at the speed of a trotting horse, and every nerve she owned remembered exactly how much one of them weighed when it hit you in the chest.

The Ember door came up like a thing on a spring.

"Six paces," said Kiva.

Her own voice startled her. It was high, and it cracked, but it was a number. "Five. It's on the line. Four."

The door stood at the threshold, straining. The bottom line warmed.

"One," she said to that, quickly, and went straight on. "Three paces. Two. On the cloth."

It was on the cloth. Its thick blunt limbs dented the waxed canvas a forearm's length from her knee, and she could smell it, which she had never been close enough to do when she was not also fighting for her life. It smelled of the air before a storm, and faintly of hot iron.

And Orla did not kill it.

She put the flat of the leaf blade against its front end, with the lit edge turned upward and away, and she held it off. She did it the way a person holds off a big dog with a broom, at the full length of her arm, her feet set wide and her weight on the good leg. The husk shoved. Kiva saw the bad knee take some of it. She saw the sinews stand out in her teacher's forearm.

Orla was not looking at the husk. Orla was looking at her.

It was the cruelest thing Orla had ever done to her, and Kiva understood it completely. A kill would have taken a second. She would have been frightened for a second and then busy. This was the half second after the surprise, stretched and stretched, with the thing scrabbling at arm's length and the woman who was holding it getting tired.

And it was not the Ember that came for her then. It was the still door.

She felt it open under her knees, heavy and sure and utterly certain of what she ought to do. *Get up. Get in front of her. Plant.* It was the door that had gone over a rail for Perrin. It did not feel like fear at all. It felt like being good.

The second line began to warm.

"Seven," said Kiva. She did not know when she had started counting the hold. "Eight. Nine. She's had it nine."

She shut the still door on the ten. She gave back ground she had never properly taken, and thought *thank you* at two hundred years of refinery brick, and felt the thing ease and go quiet. The second line cooled. Her hands had not moved. The pans were level.

"Where's your mark, catcher?" said Orla. Her voice was tight with effort and perfectly conversational.

"Half a pace to your left. It's on the corner. The fall will go off the edge."

Orla shifted half a pace to her left. The husk followed the yellow light like a compass needle, and its low heavy body swung onto the middle of the cloth.

The leaf blade turned over.

She did not thrust so much as lean. The edge went in under the blunt front, at the height of the red cord, about a hand's depth, and came out again. It was the least amount of killing Kiva had ever seen. Then the light was gone and the metal was running home and Orla was stepping back, one pace, two.

On the third, her knee folded.

She went down sideways onto one hand and one hip, hard, on the edge of the brick by the south tank. The sound she made was small and bitten off.

"*Count!*" said Orla, through her teeth.

---

The husk had already let go.

It poured down onto the canvas in front of Kiva's knees, warm, a soft grey rush that went on and on. The heap lifted and settled. Her whole body was turned toward the woman on the floor.

"One," said Kiva. "Two."

She did not decide to say it. It was simply the next number, and her mouth had it. She folded the near corners of the cloth inward with the backs of her wrists, and the heap ran together, and she set the edge of the right-hand pan beneath it.

And she felt the ash.

She had felt cells all her life. She knew a tune by its shape the way she knew a key in the dark. She had felt dead ash all week, dry and silent as sand. She had thought fresh ash would be somewhere between the two.

It was nothing like either.

It was listening. That was the only word she could find for it afterward. The whole warm heap, through the steel of the pan and the wax of the cloth, was open in every grain, like a room full of people who have all stopped talking at once and turned toward the door. It had no tune. It wanted one. It did not care whose.

And her hand door, which had gone its whole life wanting cells, went wild.

It came up in her so fast and so cold that her teeth ached. A cell already had a tune in it. A cell could be read, and nudged, and, on one disastrous afternoon, pressed. This had nothing in it at all. She could have laid a pattern on that heap as easily as laying her palm on a table, and it would have taken it, gladly, entire, and kept it until somebody threw it in the salt.

"*Six,*" said Kiva. It came out as nearly a shout. "Seven. Eight."

First pan. Up, level, over the tin. In. The hand door was at the threshold with both fists on the wood. The bottom line was warm and she had already called it. Second pan. She did not look at Orla. She could hear her breathing, short and careful, two and a half paces away. "Fourteen. Fifteen."

Her left hand was slow. The two dull fingers lagged behind the rest, and the pan in that hand rode a finger's width lower at one end than she meant it to. On the third pass, a long grey tongue of ash slid off the low end, over the hem of the cloth, and onto the bare brick.

She could not get it back. There was no time, and a pan would not lift it clean from between bricks. She let it go and it hurt to let it go.

"Twenty-one." Lid. She pressed it with the heel of her right hand and listened, and it clicked, and she ran the third finger of her left around the rim the way Mara had shown her. Flush. Cord, twice, the end pinned under her thumb. Through the loop.

"Seal check," she said, and there was nobody to check it.

"Turn it over," said Orla's voice, from the floor. "Over the cloth. Go on."

She turned it over. Nothing fell.

Second tin at thirty-six. Third at forty-nine, and her hands were shaking now, not from the door but from plain haste, and she made herself press that lid twice because the first click had sounded wrong. *Seal check.* Over. Sound.

There was still ash on the cloth. A scatter, a thin drift where the heap had stood, perhaps a quarter of what had fallen. She got the pan under some of it and had the fourth tin half filled.

"Fifty-eight," she said. "Fifty-nine. Sixty."

The listening stopped.

It did not fade. It was there and then it was not, as if every one of those turned heads had lost interest in the same instant and looked away. What lay on the cloth and the brick was only ash, grey and quiet and nobody's.

The hand door, with nothing left to want, sagged, and she shut it, and it went.

Kiva dropped the pan and scrambled the two and a half paces on her knees.

"Don't fuss," said Orla. She was sitting up, propped on one arm, with her bad leg stretched out and her face the color of the brick dust. There was sweat on her upper lip. "It held for the kill and two steps after. I asked it for three. That's my sum to do, not yours." She shut her eyes briefly. "Did you seal three?"

"Three. The fourth's sweepings."

"And what did you open?"

Kiva thought about it honestly, kneeling on the cold brick with her hands grey to the wrist. She thought about the Ember on its spring, and the still door that had felt like being good, and the hand door with its fists on the wood.

"Nothing," she said. "All three came to the doorway. The still one got half open, when you were holding it off, and I called it and shut it. One line, twice. Never two." Her voice wobbled, and she let it. "I wanted to get in front of you."

"I know you did. I was watching you want it." Orla opened her eyes. "That was the one I came down here to see. Help me up, and fetch me that stick, and then go and stand somewhere I can't see you for a minute. I believe I'm going to be rather pleased, and I'd like to do it in private."

---

The three tins weighed fourteen drams and six-tenths.

Druce did it on the big brass scale by the tall desk, with all of Team Seven crowded behind him, and he wrote the figure in the chained book in his large soft hand. *25th. Sponsor's floor, O. Dane, Kill. K. Rowan, Catch, no door. One Handful, plain. Tinned 14.6 in count. Last seal 49. Remainder to sweepings.*

"What are sweepings worth?" said Perrin.

"Very little." Druce capped his pen. "What's tinned inside the count has only itself to listen to, and it settles clean. What's still lying open at sixty settles to whatever it's lying on. In here, that's two hundred years of refinery floor. I'll sell it to the Works for road lamps at Grade Third and they'll complain about it." He glanced at Kiva. "It doesn't go toward the twenty. Yours would not have been a pass."

"No," said Kiva.

"It wasn't a test of the catch." Orla was sitting on the bench by the desk with her leg up on a stool and a cold cloth across the knee, which Anwen had fetched and laid there without asking and then read with her fingertips for a moment, frowning. "Warden. Have you a certification form in that desk, or do I have to go up to the Measure Hall on one leg?"

Druce had one. He blew the dust off it.

She wrote it out on her good knee, slowly, and Kiva watched the pencil. It took a long time for so few lines. When it was done, Orla signed it, and Druce signed below as witness and entered the number of the form in the Kennel book, and then Orla held the page out.

*I, O. Dane, accountable sponsor, certify K. Rowan (provisional, Third Division) for live work in the recovery role only: dark hands, under a declared team call, no door opened within three paces of open ash or of any charged cell.*

*Evidence. Seven nights' tin record, attached. One sponsor's floor, Kennel book, the 25th.*

*Not certified: Kill, Hold, Carry, or tuning of any kind.*

*To be reviewed after her first field response.*

Kiva read it twice.

It was so small. She had known it would be. She had told herself on the tram that morning exactly how small it would be, and she had still, somewhere underneath, been keeping a place for a different piece of paper, one that said she was safe and ready and sound. This one said she was allowed to kneel on a floor and open nothing.

"It's the lowest defensible certification," she said.

"It is." Orla did not apologize for it. "I signed for what I saw this afternoon. I didn't sign for what I hope, and I'm never going to again. You'll get the next line when you've shown me the next thing."

Kiva folded the page along its middle. Something had been sitting on her chest since Tuesday of last week, since the Cellhouse steps and the word *Saturday*, and she had got so used to the weight of it that she only knew it by its going.

"Seven's whole," said Anwen quietly. She had her list out. She drew a line through something near the top, a single firm stroke.

"A private husk," said Perrin. He was looking at the certificate over Kiva's shoulder, and his voice had the stretched sound it took on when the joke was not going to come out as a joke. "I just want to observe it. She had a private husk, killed for her, by a Torch, on a floor nobody else was allowed on. And a form with her name in it that the warden had to blow the dust off." He breathed out through his nose. "I'm pleased. I am. I held my hands on the glass the whole time, and they hurt, and I didn't notice. I'm also going to be unbearable about it until roughly supper."

"That's fair," said Kiva.

"I know it's fair. That's what makes it so tiring."

Kellan had said nothing at all. He was reading the entry in the Kennel book upside down, across the desk.

"Fourteen point six," he said. "Last seal at forty-nine. You lost a panful off the left side on the third pass, and you'd have had time to seal a fourth tin if you hadn't pressed the third lid twice." He looked up. "The mark's twenty."

"I know what the mark is."

"I'm not criticising. That's where we start from." He took the pencil from behind his ear and held it out to Druce, end first. "Warden. When can Team Seven have a floor?"

Druce took his time. He looked at the four of them, the boy with the cabbage hands, the girl with the list, the First Division band with its new white stitching, and Kiva with the form folded small in her fist.

"Tomorrow at four," he said. "All of you. And bring a sandbag. I've none to spare."

---

She stopped on the side stair, coming up, with the others gone ahead into the daylight.

There was nobody to see. She put two fingers inside her collar and drew the disk out on its cord and held it in her palm. There was still Kennel ash in three of the grooves from a fortnight ago. She had never been able to get it all out.

It was cold. It was as cold as it had been in the Old Yard at thirteen, and on the mezzanine, and in the hallway in the dark.

She had not expected anything else. She had opened no doors that afternoon, and Orla had been clear in the yard about what a sequence was. A person who had spent a whole floor doing nothing three times over had no business looking at a piece of old metal as if it owed her a comment.

She tucked it back and went up.

That night the certificate lay on the kitchen table beside the field book, and Mara read it standing, with her glasses pushed up, as she read everything from an office. She read the last line twice.

"Recovery only," she said.

"Recovery only."

"No doors near ash. No doors near cells. Reviewed after." Mara laid it down very gently and squared it with the edge of the table. "That's a meter, and a shutdown path, and a book. In four lines." She was quiet. "I'll copy it for Examiner Rusk in the morning. I never thought to be sending the Works something of Orla Dane's and be glad of it."

"She fell," said Kiva. "After the kill. Her knee went."

"Is she hurt?"

"She says not. Anwen read it. She says it's angry, and that's all." Kiva opened the book and took the pencil off its string. "She told me to put it in the column. She said she was putting it in hers."

Mara nodded, and sat, and watched her write.

*Exercise:* Sponsor's floor. Kennel. Live Handful. Orla kill, me catch. No door.

*What went wrong:* Left hand slow. Lost a panful off the cloth, third pass. Pressed third lid twice. Only 3 tins in the count.

*Who was hurt:* Orla. Knee, stepping back.

*What it cost:* About 9 drams to sweepings. Orla's knee.

*How long to come back:* Lines, none lit past one. Orla, don't know.

*Second go:* Tomorrow, 4. Whole team.

*Why:* All three doors came to the doorway and I counted through them. The still door was the worst. It didn't feel like being frightened. It felt like helping. Fresh ash listens. The hand door wants it more than it ever wanted a cell. Counting holds.

---

## CHAPTER — books/book-2680-opening/manuscript/chapter-12.md

# Chapter 12 — Kiva: Somebody's Blade

Perrin brought the sandbag on his shoulder, through the streets, all the way from Stonehand Hall.

He came down the Kennel stair at ten to four on Friday with seventy kilos of coverall draped over him like a drunken uncle, and he set it on the cast-room bench between Anwen and Kellan and patted its chest.

"This is Gerald," he said. "The Hall has four hundred of them. Nobody will miss him until the autumn count. He's had a hard life and he's about to break his leg, so I'd like everyone to be kind."

"Does he have to have a name?" said Kellan.

"The third team at Crooked Line has one called Donny. Kiva told me. I'm not having my casualty be less of a person than theirs." Perrin flexed his hands. The palms were still blotched yellow and green, and he held them a little open, like someone carrying two cups. "I can lift. I can't grip for long. If I drop him, I want it noted that he was difficult."

"Roles," said Druce, from the door.

They stood up. It was the first time Team Seven had declared anything together since a whitewashed corridor a fortnight ago, and Kiva saw that each of them had remembered it at the same moment, because nobody quite looked at anyone else.

"Renn. Kill."

"Pryce. Hold."

"Cade. Carry. And Gerald, casualty."

"Rowan. Catch." It was easier the second time. "No door. Dark hands."

"Entered." Druce wrote it in the chained book. "One Handful. The casualty will be where I put him, and I'll put him somewhere awkward. Catcher sets the mark. You've ten minutes on the floor to argue about it."

---

They did not argue for long, because Kiva already knew where she wanted it.

She took them to the strip of brick three paces from the south tank's west corner and dropped a pinch of dead ash to show them. It lay where it fell. "The falls cancel here. Anywhere else on this floor, it creeps."

"Then I kill it here," said Kellan. He had watched Selka Dray lose fifteen drams down a grate and had taken the lesson without needing it explained twice. "Pryce brings it to the cloth. I stay dark until it's on the canvas."

"I'll walk it in from the north bridge." Anwen was measuring the distance with her eyes. "Ward up at six paces, so it reads me the moment it's through the door, and I back along this line. When my heels touch the cloth, I step off to the left and it keeps coming."

"And I stand by the tank and admire you all," said Perrin, "until somebody falls over."

It went almost exactly like that.

Druce carried Gerald down himself and wedged him into the low end of the north tank, half under a plank bridge, with one leg folded beneath him in a way no leg ought to go. Then he went to the lever. The husk that came through the steel door was plain and grey and hurried, and it swung to Anwen's ward as a compass swings north.

Anwen walked it in. Kiva, kneeling at the south hem of the canvas with a pan in each hand, watched her friend come backward along the brick with her palms a foot apart and the curved silver of the ward leaning between them, and thought that she had never seen her look so entirely like herself. She did not hurry. Her heels found the edge of the cloth. She stepped off to the left.

"Five paces," said Kiva. "Four. It's on the line. Three."

The Ember came up. She called it and went on counting. It was beginning to feel less like holding a door against a dog and more like a thing her mouth did while her hands were busy.

The husk came onto the canvas after the ward. Kellan's blade lit, a hard blue-white line, and went through the knot in one short thrust from the side, and was dark again before the shape had finished letting go.

"Count!" said Anwen and Kiva, together.

The ward folded, cleanly, like a hand closing. The heap poured down warm in front of Kiva's knees, and lifted, and settled, and she set the pan beneath it.

It was listening, as the other had listened. The hand door came up cold and wild and she had a number ready for it. Corners in. First pass. The tin. She was quicker than yesterday. Her left hand still lagged, and she had learned in one evening at the kitchen table to give that hand the pan that mattered less and let the right do the lifting.

"Eleven. Twelve."

Somewhere behind her, Perrin had gone into the north tank after Gerald and was saying, quite gently, "Come on, old son. Mind your leg."

It was at about twenty that she felt the draft.

She did not know what else to call it. There was no moving air in that hall. But the listening in the ash, all those small turned heads, seemed to tilt. It was very slight. It was as if a door had been opened somewhere to her right, in another room, and a fraction of the heap's attention had gone that way to see who had come in.

She glanced up.

There was nothing there. To her right lay the south tank, and beyond it the plank bridge, and on the bridge stood Kellan, a good way off, watching the steel door. Behind him was the north gutter with its open grates.

*The grate,* she thought. *It's leaning to the grate. Everything in here runs downhill.*

She put her head down and tinned.

"Seal check," she said at twenty-six, and Anwen's thumb came down on the lid. "Sound." Thirty-three. Thirty-nine. The fourth tin took what was left on the cloth, and she ran the lazy finger round its rim and felt it flush and wound the cord.

"Forty-four," said Kiva. "Sealed. All of it. There's nothing on the brick."

"Sixty," said Druce, some time later, into a silence. "Settled."

Perrin came over the line with Gerald across his shoulders and his face shining. Anwen was already weighing the carrier in her two hands as though she could tell the drams by heft. Kiva stayed on her knees a moment longer, because her legs had stopped belonging to her.

Druce weighed the four tins on the brass scale. He did it twice.

"Twenty-one," he said. "Flat. Inside forty-four." For the first time since she had met him, something happened in his thin face that might, on another man, have been a smile. "That's over the mark, from a catcher who couldn't close a lid ten days ago. Don't let it go to your heads. The tins go in the cold room for the night, every tin, every time, and we'll see what you've got in the morning."

"Why the night?" said Perrin. He was still grinning.

"Because ash doesn't always tell you at once what's been done to it," said Druce. "Go home."

---

The cold room was a brick cellar behind the cast room, lined with slate shelves, and at eight o'clock on Saturday morning their four tins stood on a shelf by themselves with a paper tag tied to the carrier.

Druce was waiting beside them. He was not smiling.

"Put your hand on that one," he said to Kiva. "Just your hand."

She laid her palm on the lid of the second tin.

It was warm.

Nothing else in that room was warm. The slate was cold enough to ache. But the tin under her hand held a faint heat, like a cup that somebody had finished drinking from a few minutes ago, and through the steel she felt something she had hoped never to feel in ash again. There was a tune in it. It was thin and it was straight and it was sharp.

She took her hand away quickly.

"All four," said Druce. "I knew when I came in to do the morning round. They hum." He lifted the second tin, carried it to a table under the lamp where a glass bell stood ready, and unwound the cord. He set the open tin beneath the glass. "Come and look. All of you."

They looked.

The ash was grey. And through the grey, from one side of the tin to the other, as fine as a hair and as bright as a scratch on new steel, ran a thread of blue-white.

Nobody said anything. Kiva did not need to turn her head. There was only one person in Meridian whose edge-light was that color, and he was standing at her left shoulder, and she heard him stop breathing.

"I paced it after you'd gone," said Druce. His soft voice had no anger in it at all, which was worse. "From the middle of that cloth to the plank bridge where Mr. Renn was standing is two paces and a half. It looks like more, because of the tank. And his blade was lit from the moment the catch began until I called sixty. Not three counts, like the dish I showed you. Fifty-nine."

"I was holding," said Kellan.

His voice was perfectly level. Kiva had heard him sound like that once before, reading a board in the Cellhouse.

"Pryce had folded. She was inside the three paces and she had to. So nobody was lit, and nobody was watching the door, and the next one could have come through onto the catcher's back." He stopped. "I stepped off until I judged I was clear, and I lit, and I watched the door. That's what Hold is for. Somebody has to be ready."

"On this floor, I'm ready," said Druce. "That's what the lever's for. In the field, the chartered hold is ready, and they stand a great deal further off than a plank bridge." He put the lid back on the tin. "It was a good instinct, Mr. Renn. I've buried men who hadn't got it. It printed a Handful."

Kiva found her voice. It did not want to be found.

"I felt it," she said. "At about twenty. Something pulled the ash to the right. I looked up and saw the gutter, and I thought it was leaning to the grate." Her face was hot. "It was him. He was in the same line. I didn't say anything, because I thought I knew what it was."

Druce turned to her, and for a moment she thought he was going to be gentle about it.

"Inside the count, the catcher owns the floor," he said. "I told you that eleven days ago. You felt something touch your yield, and you explained it to yourself and kept quiet. Next time you feel anything, anything at all, you say *dark*, and you let them all be annoyed with you." He picked up the carrier. "There's the salt. You'll do it yourselves. I find it stays with people better."

---

Kellan Renn poured the first tin.

Nobody had asked him to go first. He took it out of the carrier because it was his, and unwound the cord that Rowan had wound, and stood over the long zinc trough of wet salt at the back of the cold room with twenty-one drams of ash that had been clean for less than a minute.

He tipped it in.

It hissed. A little steam came up, grey and sour, and the blue-white thread, which he could see quite clearly now that he knew to look for it, curled and went dull and was gone.

He had been taught to recognize the smell of that before he could read. Other people's mothers talked about damp, or the price of bread. His had talked about print. *It came in printed. Some fool stood over it with a lamp-cell. You could smell the tune.* He had sat at the kitchen table when he was six and seven and eight, doing his letters, while she told his father about tins she had failed that day and the faces of the men who had brought them. He had thought, at eight, that those men must be very stupid.

He poured the second tin.

It had been the right thing to do. That was what he kept arriving at, and it was no use at all. Every lane he had ever fought in, every master who had ever stood behind him, had put the same habit into him until it was no more a decision than blinking. You did not stand unlit on a floor with a door that might open. You finished one, and your blade stayed low and ready, and your eyes went to where the next would come from. He had held a stair that way a fortnight ago, and the booth had written it up as the best thing on the run.

He had done the best thing he knew how to do, and done it well, and it had cost the team its whole yield. There was no column on any board he had ever stood under for that.

The third tin. The fourth. Pryce had come to stand beside him, and after a moment so had Cade, and they tipped the last two between them without a word.

Twenty-one drams. A Handful, less a little. He knew to the tenth what that was worth on the Halden floor, graded Second, and he knew what it would have bought. He made himself finish the sum instead of looking away from it.

"Mine," he said. "In the book. Write it as mine."

"It's the team's floor," said Pryce.

"The print's mine. It has my light in it. I'm not having it entered as a thing that happened." He turned to the warden. "What did Nineteen tin at the river on Monday?"

Druce looked at him for a moment, as if deciding whether the question was a way of changing the subject.

"Nothing," he said. "Dray killed it on a mudbank at the turn of the tide, in under five seconds, and by the time their cloth was down, the river had it. They've been put back to thirty-ninth to try again. You're still fortieth." He did not say it unkindly. "If you were hoping that would make you feel better."

"No," said Kellan. "I wanted to know whether I was right."

He had been right. It did not make him feel better. He noted that, too, and set it aside.

Rowan was standing by the slate shelves with her hands tucked under her arms, as though they were cold. She looked as wretched as he felt, which was unreasonable of her. She had tinned twenty-one in forty-four with two bad fingers.

He went over. He did not know how to begin, so he began with the part that had a number in it.

"Two and a half paces. I'd have said four."

"So would I."

"You felt it at twenty."

"I thought it was the drain."

"Next time don't think. Say it." He heard how that sounded, and tried again. "I mean I'd rather be told. I'd rather be told wrongly ten times than not told once. If you say *dark* and it turns out to be a drain, I've lost nothing but a few seconds of standing about."

She nodded. She was looking at his right hand, and he realized that he was holding it a little away from his side, as he had in the yard on the first night of the tin.

There was a harder thing to ask, and nobody else on the team could answer it.

"What do you do?" he said. "For the sixty. When there's a door that might open, and you've nothing in your hands, and you're not allowed to be ready." It came out lower than he had intended. "I've never stood on a floor dark with a live door on it. I don't know what that is. I don't know where to put myself."

He had half expected her to be pleased. She had every right to be. She only frowned, the way she frowned at a meter, working out an honest answer.

"I count," she said. "Out loud. It sounds like nothing, I know. It's harder than it sounds, and it's the whole thing. If my mouth's got a number in it, the door stays in the doorway." She hesitated. "You could count with me. From the cut. Then if I stop, you'll know something's wrong, and if you stop, I'll know."

It was a ridiculous remedy. It was the kind of thing one taught to children who were afraid of a cellar.

He took the pencil from behind his ear and the folded paper from his pocket, the one that had been there since the Terrace Ring, and under the old lines he wrote three more.

*Rowan sets the mark. Dark at the cut, six paces, not three. Count aloud with the catcher.*

"Where do you want the next one killed?" he said.

"Same place."

"Then that's where I'll kill it." He put the paper away. "Four o'clock. The warden's given us a second floor. I asked him before you came in."

---

The second floor went wrong before the lever was pulled.

The Kennel's own kits were all out. Eleven teams were flooring on Saturday afternoon, and Druce had told them on Friday to bring one from their schools, and Perrin had said, with a wave of one green hand, that Stonehand had a spare in the stair cupboard and he would see to it.

He had seen to it. He set it down on the cast-room bench at a quarter to four, a roll of canvas and a carrier and a salt box, with the air of a man delivering a wedding cake.

Kiva unrolled the cloth. Two of the four corners had lead weights sewn into them. The other two had a few inches of unpicked thread.

"Perrin."

"It's a cloth. It's got corners."

"It's got two corners." She opened the carrier. Five tins. One of the five had a cord so old that the wax came off on her thumb in flakes. She lifted the lid of the salt box, and looked, and tilted it toward him.

The salt was white and dry and lay loose in the tin as sand.

"It's salt," said Perrin. "Salt's dry. That's what salt is. You keep it dry so it doesn't go hard."

"It's quench salt. It has to be wet through or it doesn't kill anything." Anwen had already taken the box out of Kiva's hands. She uncorked her water flask and emptied the whole of it in, and stirred with two fingers, tight-lipped. "If Kiva had needed this in the count, she'd have been pouring printed ash into a box of table salt."

"Nobody told me it had to be wet."

"The warden told all of us. On the first Tuesday. He hit it with a chalk."

"I had no hands on the first Tuesday! I was in a great deal of discomfort!" Perrin looked at the three of them and deflated. "All right. That's mine. I'll write it down. *What went wrong: Perrin. Why: Perrin.* She's got a page of those already."

Kellan had said nothing. He had gone out, and he came back with two loose bricks from the lip of the cold-room step, one in each hand, and set them on the bench beside the unweighted corners.

"Four tins is a Handful," he said. "The fifth's spare. Use the bad cord last." He glanced at Perrin. "I printed twenty-one drams yesterday. I'm not in a position to say anything about salt."

It was, Kiva thought, very nearly a joke. Perrin stared at him as if a lamp-post had told him the time.

---

"One Handful," said Druce. "The casualty's in the south tank this time. Catcher, is your mark the same?"

"The same."

"Then mind it. I've a feeling about today."

He pulled the lever, and the thing that came through the steel door had a skin on it.

Kiva saw it at once, because she had been looking for it ever since the Wednesday before last, and her stomach went cold. The same faint sheen lay over the blunt front and back across the shoulders, like the bloom on a grape. It swung toward Anwen's ward and came on.

"It's got the skin," she said. "Kellan. It's a skin one."

"Seen," said Kellan. He was standing dark, six paces off, with his empty fist at his side. "Same plan. I'll go through it."

Anwen walked it in. Its front end met her ward at the third pace, a little sooner than the plain one had, and where the two touched, there came a high thin ringing, silver on silver, like the rim of a glass being rubbed with a wet finger. Anwen's jaw set. Her heels found the canvas. She stepped left.

The husk did not keep coming. It turned with her, onto the edge of the cloth, still pushing at the ward.

Kellan lit.

The husk's front end swung round to him so fast that its hind limbs skidded. He was already thrusting, from the side, at the height of the red cord, and his blade struck the turning shoulder and skated off with a shriek. He cut again, back-handed. It rang again and turned. The thing drove at him, and he gave ground toward the tank, and Anwen brought her ward round to cover him, and the husk swung back to *that*, and Kiva, on her knees with nothing but two pans, saw the whole of it laid out as plainly as a diagram on a slate.

It turned its skin to whatever was brightest. Blade lit, it faced the blade. Blade dark for a breath, it faced the ward.

It would never show Kellan its side as long as he was lit.

"It's facing what's lit!" she shouted. "It turned to you when you lit. It goes back to her when you don't—"

The husk shoved. Anwen went back a pace and a half on the brick, and her left heel came down on the lip of the south tank, and there was nothing behind it.

"*Off!*" said Anwen. Her voice cracked across the hall like a slate breaking. "Off! Off!"

Everything went dark.

Kiva had not known how complete it would be. The ward folded. The blue-white line vanished. Perrin, who had been knee-deep in the south tank with Gerald's collar in his fist, let go of the ground with a grunt she felt through her shins, and hauled the sandbag up over the lip on plain muscle. And the husk, which had been driving at Anwen with its whole weight, simply stopped.

It stood on the corner of the canvas. Its blind front end moved a little, left and right, like a hand feeling along a shelf in the dark.

"Walk," said Kellan, very quietly. "Don't run. Round it. Pryce first."

They walked. Kiva gathered the pans and left the tins. She went past the thing at a distance of four paces with her heart going like a pump, and it paid her no more attention than it paid the brick. Behind her, Perrin came with Gerald across his shoulders, breathing through his teeth.

By the time they reached the yellow stripe at the west end, the husk had found something else. It had turned toward the steel door it had come from, and the lattice that sat behind that door, and was pressing its skinned face against the metal like a dog asking to be let back in.

Druce had his hand on the lever and had not moved it.

"That," he said, "is the first thing any of you have done on this floor that I would pay for."

Anwen was white. She was holding her left wrist in her right hand. "I should have called it sooner. When it turned with me on the cloth. I knew then."

"You called it before anyone went in the tank. That's sooner than most." He looked at the four of them. "You've a husk on the floor and it's going nowhere. You may walk off now, and I'll enter a clean abort, and that's a respectable day. Or you can tell me in two minutes what you intend to do differently, and go back in. I won't think the less of you either way."

Nobody looked at anybody.

"It can't face two ways," said Kiva.

---

It took them the whole two minutes, and most of it was Kellan asking her questions.

How fast did it turn? Faster than he could cut twice. Did it turn to a ward as quickly as to a blade? No, she thought, a hair slower, but she had only seen it twice. If Pryce's ward was up and his blade was dark, where was its side? Toward wherever he chose to stand.

"Then I come in dark," he said. "From behind its shoulder. I light at the stroke and not before. One cut, down behind the skin, into the knot."

"You've never lit at the stroke," said Anwen. "You light and then you step. I've watched you. It's always light, then step."

He considered that as if she had read him a meter. "Yes. I'll have to not."

"And it has to be on the cloth," said Kiva. "All of it, not the corner. Anwen, it followed you when you stepped off. If you step off, it'll walk off after you. You'll have to stay on the canvas and let it come right up."

"With my heels on your tins."

"I'll move the tins."

Perrin raised a hand. "And I stand by the tank, with Gerald, and if anybody says *off*, I pick up whoever's nearest. I only want it said that somebody thought about me."

They went back in.

Anwen raised her ward at eight paces, and the husk left the steel door at once and came to it, ringing. She walked it down the hall. She walked it onto the cloth, and across it, until her heels were at the south hem and the whole grey length of the thing stood on waxed canvas with its skinned front pressed to her ward, a pace and a half from Kiva's left knee.

Kiva could hear Anwen breathing. She could hear the ward, a thin continuous note.

Kellan was coming up the brick behind it. He was dark. He walked on his own feet, with his right fist held low and a little forward, and he looked like a person crossing a room full of sleeping people.

Three paces. Two.

His shoulder dropped. And his blade lit, a hard white flash, before his foot had come down.

It was a breath early. It was the work of ten years, and it was no more than half a breath, and it was enough. The husk's front end began to come round.

He cut anyway. He had no other choice left. The edge came down behind the turning shoulder and caught the rim of the skin and the knot beneath it in the same stroke.

The skin burst.

It did not fall like the plate Selka Dray had broken. It flew. A thin bright curve stood in the air for an instant and then went outward in a spray, and the husk let go underneath it, and ash went everywhere. It poured down on the canvas. It fanned out across the brick to the south. A long plume of it went over the lip of the tank.

"Dark!" said Kiva. "*Dark!* One. Two."

"One," said Kellan. "Two." His blade was gone. He was backing away with his empty hands held open at his sides, six paces, counting in a flat hard voice like a man reading out a sentence. "Three."

She worked.

It was a bad catch. Half the fall lay on the cloth, and she had that tinned by twenty-five. The rest was a grey scatter on bare brick, and she went after it on her knees with the edge of the pan and the brush, which was not what the brush was for, and got what she could. Her left hand dropped a panful. She swore, and said "thirty-one," and went on.

And under the listening, there was something else.

She noticed it on the second tin and thought she was imagining it. By the third she was sure. Most of the ash was as the others had been, wide open and waiting. But in among it, like a few grains of rice in a bowl of flour, there was ash that was not waiting. It already had something in it, very faint. It was not a tune, and it was certainly not a blade. It was a kind of lean, a slight held curve, like a ward that somebody had folded badly and put away.

Nobody was lit. She checked, with her eyes, between counts. Kellan was six paces off with his hands open. Anwen was against the far wall. Perrin was a statue beside the tank.

"Fifty-seven," said Kiva, and pressed the last lid. "Seal check."

"Sound," said Anwen, who had come in at fifty with her hands behind her back to put a thumb on it.

"Sixty. Settled." Druce had left the lever. He was walking down the hall toward them with the chained book under his arm, and his eyes were on the four tins.

They weighed fifteen drams and three-tenths. It was under the mark by nearly five.

"That's the skin," said Druce. "It goes off like a dropped lamp. I've not seen one tinned above sixteen." He uncapped his pen. "*Fourth this month.*"

"Warden." Kiva had not got up. She was still looking at the tins. "Some of it had something in it already. Before I touched it. Nobody was lit, I looked. It wasn't a print. It was only in a little of it, the part that came off the front." She tried to find the word, and could only find the one she had. "It felt curved."

Druce's pen stopped.

"Say that again," he said. "Slowly. And then say it a third time, exactly the same, while I write."

---

The Pryce laundry kept its coppers going on Saturday night, and the back room behind them was the warmest place on Coper Street.

Anwen had commandeered it. She sat on an upturned basket with her list flat on the mangle table, and her mother had come through twice with bread and once with a look, and had gone away again when she saw four heads bent over a pencil.

"The warden says it's Hollis Vane who has the Sag Line watch this month," said Anwen. "Home Line. And he says Vane does the same thing to every team that comes onto a field hold, and has done for ten years." She wrote a heading and underlined it. "He takes one of you away. In the middle. He points and says *you're down*, and whoever it is lies where they are, and the rest of the team finds out whether it was a team or four people standing near each other."

"That's vile," said Perrin, with his mouth full.

"That's a real rift," said Kiva. She was thinking of a boy on his back under a shutter. "People go down."

"So we plan for each." Anwen wrote *RENN* and looked up.

Kellan was sitting very straight on a soap crate. He had a tin in his lap. All evening, whenever nobody was speaking to him, he had been standing in the corner by the drying racks with his hands open at his sides, and Kiva had heard him, under the rumble of the coppers, counting to sixty.

"If I'm down, you call it off," he said. "At once. Neither of you can cut a knot, and Cade's hands won't break one. You carry me to the line, and we go in again when he lets me up." He said it without any vanity, the way he would have reported a missing part. "Don't try to finish it without a blade. That's how the abort nobody called happens."

*Renn down: OFF. Cade carries.*

"If I'm down," said Anwen, "there's no ward to walk it in. Kellan would have to bring it to the cloth himself, lit, and then go dark in front of it, and then light again." She tapped the pencil against her teeth. "That's too many changes with a thing that turns that fast. Off."

*Pryce down: OFF.*

"If I'm down," said Kiva slowly, "somebody else catches."

They all looked at Perrin.

Perrin put down his bread. "I'd like to draw the room's attention to my hands."

"You can press a lid with the heel," said Kiva. "I do. And you'd have Anwen for the cord." She pushed the carrier across the table. "You've got until we're called. Six tins. I'll count."

He did eleven before midnight, with his tongue between his teeth and his big green palms held stiffly away from the wax, and Kiva said *seal check* for him and turned each one over. Four came open. He looked at the grey dust on the laundry floor as though it had personally let him down.

"A street lamp for a fortnight," he said mournfully. "I see it now. I see what he means."

"And if you're down?" said Kellan.

"Nobody can carry me," said Perrin. "I've thought about it. I weigh as much as Gerald and a half. So if I'm down, you'll all learn something very important about leaving a friend where he fell." He picked up the twelfth tin. "Drag me by the feet. I shan't mind. I'll be dead."

*Cade down: drag.*

Anwen blotted the list and folded it once. Kiva watched her put it in her coat, in the inside pocket, and thought of a seat on Team Eleven that a field officer was holding until a bell rang.

Anwen saw her thinking it.

"Seven's whole," she said. "I crossed it out. I don't write things down twice."

---

The knock came at half past three in the morning, and it was on the shop door.

Kiva was awake before she knew why. She lay in the dark and heard it again, three flat blows of a fist on wood, and then Mara's feet on the stairs, quick and unsurprised, the tread of a woman who had been got out of bed for cells all her working life.

She went down after her in her nightshirt, with the heat-lines pulling as she reached for the rail.

There was a courier on the step, a girl no older than Kiva in an undyed canvas coat with a tar-black band on the sleeve. A lift-truck stood humming at the curb behind her with its rings lit.

"Rowan Instruments? You keep the book for mast six on the Sag Line."

"I keep the instrument," said Mara. She had a lamp in one hand. "What's it done?"

"It rang at ten to three. There's a tear in the allotments under it, small, and the Home Line's got a hold round it. But six rang a good four minutes before five and seven did, and the officer won't trust any of the three until somebody who knows that mast has looked at its drum." The girl held out a folded paper. "He says bring the book. He says he doesn't want it tuned. He wants it read."

Kiva saw her aunt's left thumb move across her right palm, once.

"He's in luck," said Mara dryly. "Reading's all I'm licensed for. Give me five minutes."

"There's a second paper." The courier was already looking past her, at Kiva on the stairs. "From the Compact. If there's a Rowan, K., at this address. Third group's called for the field license, twenty-seven through forty. Muster at the plank bridge at first light, with kit."

For a moment nobody moved. The lift-truck hummed. Somewhere down Coper Street a second fist was knocking on a second door.

Then, through the wall, Kiva heard a window go up in the flat over the laundry, and a voice she had known since she was nine say, clear and wide awake, "I heard. I'm dressed. Tell her I'm coming down."

The bell had rung.

And Anwen was coming down.

---

## CHAPTER — books/book-2680-opening/manuscript/chapter-13.md

# Chapter 13 — Kiva: License Day

The tear was not dark.

Kiva had always imagined that it would be. Every drawing in every schoolbook showed a rift as a black slit, like a cut in a curtain. The thing that hung over Agnes Roake's potato bed at first light on Sunday was the color the sky goes in the last minute before the street lamps come on, a deep, clear, tired blue, and it was no bigger than a cellar door laid on its side. It floated at about the height of her knee. The air around its edge shivered the way air shivers over a stove.

She stood on the Sag Line road with the kit roll under her arm, sixty paces off and four above, and felt it in the back of her neck like a thumb.

Under her shirt, the three lines lay quiet. She had half expected them to answer it. They did not, and she was grateful, and a little ashamed of being grateful.

"Small," said Mara, beside her. She had the mast's book in one hand and her tool roll in the other, and she was looking at the tear as she would have looked at a fault in someone else's wiring. "I've seen four. That's the smallest. It'll be making Handfuls and nothing else." She turned her back on it. "Go and find your team. I've a drum to read."

The allotments ran south from the foot of the berm in long narrow strips, grey with dew, each with its shed and its water butt and its rows of poles standing ready for beans that had not yet been planted. A drainage cut two paces wide ran along the bottom of the berm between the road and the plots. Somebody had staked a square of white tape around the corner plot, twenty paces to a side, and at each corner of the square stood a figure in an undyed canvas coat with both hands raised.

Between those four pairs of hands, faint as breath on a window, hung four walls of silver.

Kiva had seen Anwen hold a ward the size of a dinner plate. She had seen Ysra Crest's name in a story about a water main. She had never seen four people hold one twenty paces long, in the open, in the cold, and from the set of their shoulders they had been doing it since three in the morning.

Inside the square were the tear, a tarred shed, a strip of cinder path, and the dug black earth of the potato bed. Nothing else moved.

"Rowan Instruments?"

The man who had come up the berm steps behind them was lean and about forty-five, with a face so weathered that his eyes looked pale in it. He wore the same canvas coat with the same tar-black band. A slate hung from his belt on a cord, and there was a stub of chalk behind his ear. He did not raise his voice. Kiva noticed that everyone near him had lowered theirs.

"Hollis Vane. I've the watch." He nodded at the steel box at the foot of mast six. "That's the one that rang first. I want to know whether to thank it or pull it out."

---

Mara had the box open in under a minute.

She unpinned the drum roll and laid it along the lid, and beside it she laid two more, which Vane's courier had brought down the line from masts five and seven. She weighted the corners with her wrenches. Kiva had seen her do exactly that on a cold bright Thursday a little more than three weeks ago, in this same place, with four rolls and nobody watching.

"There," said Mara. She set her finger on the inked line of mast six. It ran flat along the roll, a little under halfway between the second grid mark and the third, and then it jumped. "Ten to three. Clean rise, no stutter. And here." She moved to the other two. Their lines ran flat exactly on the second mark, and jumped in the same shape. "Six minutes to. Both of them. Four minutes behind."

"Five and seven were recalibrated in the winter," said Vane. "Wren House sent someone. It's in their books. Six wasn't."

"No."

"Why not?"

Mara turned the mast's cell book round on the lid and opened it to the entry. She did not explain it. She let him read.

*Baseline elevated, consistent with prior quarters. Cell clean. Drum true. No fault found. Recal. would mask trend. Not recalibrated. M.R.*

Hollis Vane read it without hurry.

"So it's reading high," he said.

"It's reading what's there." Mara's voice was level. "The other two are reading what's there, less a correction somebody put in to make the line sit where the manual says it ought to. The bell's set to a mark on the grid. Drop the baseline, and the line has further to climb before it rings. I didn't think of four minutes when I wrote that. I only thought that if I flattened it, nobody would see it creeping." She took her finger off the paper. "I ought to tell you that my tuning license is under review with the Works. If you'd rather somebody else read your mast."

"I didn't ask who tuned it. I asked who left it alone."

He unhooked the slate from his belt and wrote on it, three lines, in a quick slanting hand.

"My first ward-holder reached that north corner about ninety seconds before the first husk stood up," he said, writing. "I timed it, afterward, because I'd thought we were going to lose it. With five and seven alone, I'd have had no hold. I'd have had a Handful loose in forty allotments in the dark, and a cold-cupboard cell in every shed that's got one." He looked up. "Rowan, M. I'll want that in the report. Whoever is reviewing you may have a copy, if a copy's any use."

Mara did not answer at once. Kiva, who knew her aunt's face better than any other thing in the world, saw the corner of her mouth tighten the way it did when a fault turned out to be exactly where she had said.

"It might be," said Mara. "Thank you. I'll write up the drum."

She bent over the rolls again. Vane hooked the slate back on his belt, and his pale eyes went to the kit under Kiva's arm, and then to her face.

"One of mine?"

"Team Seven."

"Last slot." He said it without sympathy and without anything else. "You'll have a long day to watch other people's mistakes. I'd use it. The hold comes down at six this evening whether you've had your turn or not. My people have been leaning on that tear since before you were awake, and there's a closing crew on its way from Brickfields who'll want it shut by dark."

"How often does it make one?"

"Every forty minutes, about. It's been regular as a tram." He started down the steps toward the plank bridge. "So far."

---

By noon she had filled two pages.

Fourteen teams had mustered at the plank bridge, and a fifteenth with one new face in it, and they sat in a long row on the grass of the berm with their kits between their feet, like passengers waiting for a ferry. Each team went in through a gap that the western ward-holders opened by lowering their hands. Each declared its roles to Hollis Vane. Each had one husk.

Kiva wrote down every fall.

She had not planned to. She had opened the field book to make a note of the wind, which at six in the morning was no wind at all, and then the first team had gone in, and its Ember had cut the knot on the cinder path, and she had found her pencil drawing a little square for the plot and a cross where the ash came down. After that she could not have stopped. She put a cross for every kill and an arrow for which way it ran, and beside each a count and a word.

*T27. Path, by shed. Still air. Tinned in 52. Clean.*

*T28. Potato bed. Soft. Husk sank to the hocks, slow. Ash fell in the furrows. Catcher dug for it. Count 60+, sweepings.*

*T29. Path. Vane said "Lund, you're down" to their ward. They kept going without her. Ember got knocked flat. Off called late. Nobody hurt much.*

"He did it again," said Perrin, on the fourth. He was lying on his back in the grass with his hands on his chest and a dock leaf over his eyes. "I heard it. *You're down.* Who was it?"

"Their catcher."

"What did they do?"

"The Carry picked up the pans." Kiva wrote. "He was slow. Thirty-one in the count before the first tin. But they got fourteen."

"I'd have got fifteen," said Perrin, to the dock leaf.

Kellan sat a little apart, with his arms round his knees, watching the blades. He had said perhaps ten words since dawn. Once, when a Stonehand team's Ember had finished a husk and stepped back with her edge still lit, Kiva had seen his whole body lean forward, and had seen him lean back again when Vane's voice came flat across the plots: "Dark, Miss Corwen. You're a lamp."

Anwen was not sitting with them at all. She was at the white tape, as close as the hold would let her stand, and she had not taken her eyes off the four ward-holders for an hour.

A man in slate blue had come down the berm steps at eight, bald and brown and unhurried, and had stood beside her for a minute. Kiva had been too far away to hear it. She had seen Doran Mace ask something, and seen Anwen answer in one word, and seen him nod and walk on along the row to where Team Eleven sat with their Wren House reserve.

The one word had been short. From where Kiva sat, it had looked a good deal like *Seven*.

---

The wind got up a little after two.

She felt it first on the back of her neck, and then she saw it, in the dead grass along the cut, all leaning the same way. It came from the west. It came down the length of the berm like water down a gutter, along the cut and over the plots, and it did not gust. It simply leaned, steadily, on everything.

She licked her pencil and wrote *Wind W to E, 2 o'clock, steady,* and then she looked east to see where it was going.

Forty paces beyond the white tape, on the bank of the cut, stood a small brick hut with a tin roof. A fat iron pipe ran from it down into the water and another ran from it up to a tank on stilts. It had been thumping quietly to itself all morning, a slow heartbeat that she had stopped hearing.

"Is that a pump?" she said.

"That's my pump," said a voice above her.

An old woman was standing on the berm behind the row of candidates, in a man's overcoat tied round the middle with string. She had been there since before sunrise, Kiva realized. She had taken her for one of the Home Line's people. She had a face like a walnut and a trowel sticking out of one pocket.

"That's the allotment pump, and I'm the pump warden this year, for my sins. And that's my potato bed they've got their door hanging over, and my shed, and those are my bean poles." She did not sound angry. She sounded like somebody reading out an inventory after a flood. "Agnes Roake. Plot nineteen. I had four hundred seedlings pricked out to go in this week. They're in trays behind the shed. I'd be obliged if nobody trod on them."

"I'll tell my team," said Kiva.

"You do that." Agnes Roake looked down at the open book on Kiva's knee, at the little squares and crosses. "What's the pencil for?"

"Where they fall. I'm the one who has to pick it up."

"Hm," said Agnes Roake. It sounded exactly like Warden Druce. "Then you'll want to know the path's firm as far as the shed and soft after. I put two barrows of cinder down in the autumn and ran out."

Kiva wrote that down too.

Team Nineteen went in at half past three, thirteenth of fourteen.

She knew before the gap closed behind them how it would go, and she hated knowing. Selka Dray walked to the north end of the plot, to the bank of the cut, which was the nearest firm ground to the tear. She bounced on her toes. Her catcher laid the cloth on the cinder path, by the shed, as nine catchers before him had done, and looked at it, and looked at Dray, and said something. Dray shook her head.

The husk stood up out of the blue at twenty to four, and Dray lit.

It took her four seconds. It was the same lovely four seconds. The thing came for her in a line straight across the dug earth, and she met it on the bank in one Stride and cut the knot at a run, and the whole grey shape let go on the very lip of the cut, in the wind.

It did not pour. The wind took it.

Kiva watched a Handful of open ash go up like smoke off a bonfire. Half of it sheeted down into the brown water and was gone. The rest streamed east along the bank in a long low grey scarf, over the white tape, through the silver of the hold as if the ward were not there at all. And it did not thin out and settle on the grass, as dust ought to. It gathered. It drew in on itself as it went, narrowing, like a thread being pulled through the eye of a needle.

The eye of the needle was a small brick hut with a tin roof.

"*Dark!*" That was Vane, and for the first time all day it was a shout. "Dark, Dray, and get your catcher to that pump house—"

It was far too late. The grey scarf went in under the eaves of the tin roof. The slow thumping heartbeat ran on for three more beats, and then it stumbled, and raced, and stopped.

The allotments were very quiet.

"There now," said Agnes Roake softly.

---

Hollis Vane wrote the result on his slate where everyone could watch him do it. Then he read it out.

"Team Nineteen. Kill, four seconds from sight. Yield, none. One civic device fouled. Response failed." He hung the slate on his belt. "Nobody hurt. I'll say that first, because it's worth more than the rest. Miss Dray, that was as quick a blade as I've seen this year."

Selka Dray was standing at the gap in the tape with her fists clenched and her face scarlet. "I *killed* it."

"You did. Now come and see what else you did. All of you who haven't been in yet. Seven, Eleven. Bring your catchers."

He walked them along the bank of the cut to the pump house, twenty-odd candidates in a straggling line, with Agnes Roake stumping along behind in her overcoat. A Wren House tuner in a grey apron was already there, kneeling at the open door with a lamp.

The cell was no bigger than the one in a tram. It sat in a cradle of copper coils beside the silent pump, and the little meter on its case was doing a thing Kiva had never seen a meter do. The needle was wandering. It drifted up to three-quarters, hung, sank to a half, and crept up again, like a drunk man trying to find a curb.

She was two paces from the door, with her hands behind her back, and she could feel it anyway.

It had been a plain municipal tune, a slow, even pump tune that some yard operator had laid in years ago and that had gone on thumping ever since. Now there was a streak through it. It was hot and quick and yellow, and it was the exact color of Selka Dray's edge-light. The tune was trying to hold its shape around it and could not, in the way a woven thing cannot hold its shape around a snapped thread.

"That's a lit blade in a pump cell," said Vane. "A few drams of it. It was open when she cut it, and she stood over it lit, and the wind carried it to the nearest pattern, and the pattern took it in. You can't press that out. You can't tune round it." He nodded to the tuner. "Go on."

The tuner broke the wire seal and pulled the red handle. There was a clank and a hiss.

"Four hundred seedlings," said Agnes Roake, "and a bucket." She looked at Selka Dray, not unkindly. "You'll not have known. I don't suppose anyone told you where the wind went."

Dray opened her mouth. She shut it again. Her catcher was standing behind her with the empty carrier, and Kiva saw him look at the cloth he had laid on the path, forty paces back, with nothing on it.

"The casualty reserve will refill that cell," said Vane. "In about a month, when the claim's been through three offices. In the meantime, plot nineteen through plot fifty-eight carry their water from the cut." He turned so that he was speaking to all of them. "The Home Line took the kill column out of its book before I was born. We keep four. Was it closed. Who was hurt. What still works in the morning. What we tinned. That order. The districts let us stand in their potato beds for exactly as long as their pumps still run when we've gone, and not one day longer." He looked along the line of faces and stopped, very briefly, at Kellan's. "Seven. You're next. You've about half an hour."

---

Kiva used the half hour.

She went in through the gap as soon as Vane would let her, with the cloth under her arm and a twist of dead ash from the Kennel in her pocket, and she walked the plot the way Orla had taught her to walk the lane.

The tear hung over the middle of the potato bed. The earth beneath it had been dug that week and was as soft as cake. South of the bed stood the tarred shed, with its back to the west and the wind. The cinder path ran north and south along the shed's eastern side, firm as far as the shed's far corner, exactly as Agnes Roake had said, and soft beyond.

She stood in the lee of the shed and dropped a pinch.

It fell straight down. She took two steps north, past the corner, and dropped another, and the wind had it before it reached her knee.

"Here," she said. "On the path, against the east wall. The shed breaks the wind. It's the only place inside the tape that does."

Kellan had come in behind her. He was not looking at the path. He was looking at the potato bed, and he went and set one foot on it, and watched his boot sink to the laces.

"I can't Stride on that."

"No."

"It's the sand again. Worse." He drew his foot out. There was no complaint in it. He was reading a meter. "And I've three paces of cinder to come up on, from the shed's north corner, and it'll have to walk past the end of that corner to get onto your cloth. So it'll be turning as it comes. Pryce will have to hold its face square the whole way down."

"I can hold it," said Anwen. She was pale, and she had her list folded very small in one fist, and she had been watching four Torch-rank ward-holders for nine hours. "I've been looking at how they lean. They don't stand behind it. They stand under it. I think I've been holding mine like a tray as well."

"And I'm by the bean poles," said Perrin. He had sewn the missing weights into the Stonehand cloth himself, on Saturday night, with his mother's needle, and he had shown the stitching to everyone on the berm who would look. "With nobody to carry, I hope. I notice he hasn't given us a Gerald."

"He doesn't use a sandbag," said Kiva. "He uses us."

They all looked across the plot, to where Hollis Vane stood at the tape with his chalk.

"Roles," he said.

"Renn. Kill."

"Pryce. Hold."

"Cade. Carry."

"Rowan. Catch." She drew a breath. "Door declared: none."

Vane's chalk paused.

"None?"

"None. Dark hands. It's the only thing I'm certified for." She had the form in her coat and held it out. He read it at arm's length, and she saw his eyes stop on the signature.

"Dane," he said. "Orla Dane. She took me down my first culvert, when I was nineteen and knew everything." Something passed over the weathered face that was nearly an expression. "She fell in it." He handed the paper back. "That's the first declaration I've had today that I entirely believe. Places."

---

The husk stood up out of the blue at twenty-two minutes past four.

It came the way they came up the chute, in a heap, and it found its legs as it rose. It had a skin. Kiva had known that it would. She could not have said why. The faint bloom lay over its blunt front like frost on a turnip.

"Skin," she said.

"Seen," said Kellan, from the shed's north corner. His hands were open at his sides.

Anwen's ward came up. It was not flat, and it was not held out in front of her. She had her hands low and wide and her weight forward, and the silver leaned from her like a door she was holding shut with her shoulder.

The husk read it and came. It went into the soft earth of the bed to its hocks, as the others had, and plowed forward regardless, and its skinned face met the ward at the edge of the cinders with that thin glass-rim note.

"Seven paces," said Kiva. She was on her knees at the east hem of the cloth, with the bean poles at her back. "Six. It's on the path."

"Renn," said Hollis Vane, from the tape. He did not raise his voice. "You're down. Right leg, at the knee."

Nobody moved.

Kiva saw it go through Kellan like a current. He was the best blade in his year. He was standing with a clean line to the thing's shoulder, and he had spent a day and a night learning how to cut it. For one long instant she watched him not sit down.

Then he sat down. He did it all at once, on the cinders, with his right leg straight in front of him, and his face shut like a book.

"*Off!*" said Anwen. "Off! Off!"

The ward folded. Kiva was already rolling the cloth, pans inside it, because a cloth left on the ground was a cloth a husk would walk across. Perrin went past her at a heavy run.

"I can walk," said Kellan.

"You're down." Perrin had him by one arm and the belt. He bent his knees, and Kiva felt the ground take the weight as the still door opened under him, and Kellan Renn went up across those broad shoulders like a rolled carpet. "Down people don't have opinions. I've wanted to do this for two weeks. Keep your leg straight, you're meant to have broken it."

"Cade—"

"Quiet. You're a casualty. Casualties groan."

They went out through the gap in a line, Anwen first and Kiva last with the kit in her arms. Behind them, the husk had already lost them. It stood on the cinder path with its blind face swinging, and then it found the nearest of the four silver walls and went and leaned on it, patiently, like a cow at a gate.

Perrin set Kellan down on the grass outside the tape with a great deal more care than he had picked him up.

Hollis Vane was looking at his slate.

"Nine seconds," he said. "From *down* to *off*. I had a Second Division team this morning who took forty, and tried to finish it with a ward and a still-door and a prayer." He chalked a line. "Whose plan was that?"

"Hers," said Kellan, getting up. He nodded at Anwen. "She wrote it out last night. I told her what to put beside my name."

"Did you." Vane considered him. "Your leg's mended, Mr. Renn. I'd say you've until that thing wears a hole in my south corner, and she's been standing there since three. In."

---

They went in.

Kiva laid the cloth again, in the lee of the shed, squared against the tarred boards. She set out four tins and the spare. She put the wet salt where her right hand would find it without looking.

Then she knelt and put a pan in each hand, and Anwen raised her ward.

The husk came off the south wall as if it had been called. It came up the cinder path toward the brighter silver with its skin shining dully in the long light, and the ward rang, and Anwen began to walk backward, slowly, with her weight under it.

It would pass the end of the shed. It would pass Kiva. She had laid the cloth so that it must.

"Five paces," she said. "Four."

It was very much bigger out of doors. She did not know why that should be. In the Kennel there had been brick all round and a warden at a lever. Here there was grass, and a tin roof, and somebody's seedlings in trays, and a blind grey thing the size of a mastiff walking down a garden path toward her with nobody's hand on anything.

The Ember came up. She had a number in its way before it reached the door.

"Three. One line. Two paces. It's at the corner—"

It was at the corner. It drew level with the shed's north end, where Kellan stood flat against the tar with his fist down, and it did not so much as flicker toward him. It walked on. Its thick limb came down on the waxed canvas a forearm from her knee, and the cloth dented.

"On," said Kiva. "All four feet. It's on."

Anwen stopped. Her heels were at the south hem. She did not step off. She stood, with a husk's face pressed to her ward an arm's length from her own, and held its eyes.

Kellan left the corner.

He came up the three paces of cinder behind it, dark. Kiva watched his feet. The left came down. The right came down. His shoulder dropped. And she saw, because she was watching the part that moved least, the precise instant when ten years of habit reached for the light and did not get it.

His foot landed.

Then the blade lit.

It was a short hard blue-white line and it was already moving. It went down behind the edge of the skin, where the grey was only grey, and into the place where the red cord would have been, and it was dark again and running home before the husk had begun to turn.

The skin did not burst. It stood for a heartbeat by itself, a bright shallow curve like the lid of a pot, with nothing under it. Then it dropped straight down and broke on the canvas, and the rest of the husk poured after it, warm, in a heap, in the middle of the cloth.

"*Count!*"

"Dark," said Kiva. "One. Two."

"One. Two." Kellan was backing away up the path. Six paces. His hands were open. "Three."

Anwen's ward folded like a hand closing. She dropped to her knees at the south hem, and her fingers were already on the corner weights.
