The heap lifted and settled. It was listening. The hand door rose cold and wild and Kiva gave it *four* and *five* and set the pan beneath the ash, and for the space of six counts it was exactly like the Kennel, and she thought, with a rush of something very like joy, *I can do this.*

She heard herself think it. She knew what came after that sort of thought.

"Six," she said, louder. "Seven."

The wind came round the shed.

It was not the wind itself. It was a curl of it, an eddy off the north corner, and it lifted the top of the heap in a thin grey veil that stood up off the canvas and began to lean toward the east.

"Corners!" said Kiva. "Up, all four, make a bag of it—Perrin! I need a wall. West side. Stand there. No. There."

"I have trained," said Perrin, planting himself between the shed corner and the cloth with his coat held open like a pair of wings, "my entire life. For this."

The veil sank. Anwen had two corners gathered in her fists and Kiva had the third under her knee. She panned out of the canvas as out of a sack. First tin at sixteen. "Seal check."

"Sound."

Second at twenty-seven. Her left hand was slow, and she let it be slow. The skin ash was in there. She could feel it, a few grains in every panful, already faintly curved. She did not stop to think about that. She had a number.

"Thirty," she said. "Thirty-one—"

"Seven." Hollis Vane's voice came across the plot, and there was an edge in it she had not heard all day. "There's a second one coming. It's early. It's not yours. Hold your catch."

Kiva looked up.

The blue over the potato bed was bulging.

---

It had been forty minutes between them since dawn. She had written it in the book thirteen times. This one had come in eleven.

It heaped out onto the dug earth and stood. It had no skin. It was plain and grey and it swung its blind front end in a slow half circle, reading.

There was nothing lit inside the tape. Anwen was dark. Kellan was dark. Perrin was a boy with his coat open. And so the husk's face went on turning until it found the nearest pattern there was, which was the silver wall on the east side of the hold and the ward-holder behind it, at the corner past the bean poles.

It started toward her.

A straight line from the tear to that corner ran across the potato bed and across the cinder path. It ran across the middle of Agnes Roake's cloth.

"Thirty-three," said Kiva. Her voice had gone up. "Thirty-four."

She saw Kellan see it. He was six paces up the path, on the far side of the line, and he had only to raise his fist. A lit blade at six paces would be the brightest thing in that field. The husk would turn to him like a needle to north. He could meet it on firm cinder with his feet set, and it had no skin, and it would take him one breath.

And for that one breath there would be a lit Edge six paces from eight drams of open ash, with the wind behind it.

His right hand had started to come up.

"*Dark,*" said Kiva. She did not shout it. She said it straight at him. "Stay dark. Thirty-five. Kellan. Count."

His hand stopped at his hip.

It shook. She saw it shake.

"Thirty-six," said Kellan Renn, through his teeth. His hand opened. "Thirty-seven."

The husk came on across the bed. It was slow in the soft earth. Somewhere outside the tape a Home Line blade was running along the east side toward the corner, a woman with her coat flying, and she was not going to be in time to do anything about a cloth.

"Kiva." Perrin had dropped his coat. He stepped over the hem of the canvas, between her and the potato bed, and stood there with his back to her. "I'm standing in front of you now. You're being rescued. I said I'd like to be told. I thought you would too."

"Perrin, you can't plant, you'll print it—"

"I know I can't plant. I'm not planting." He sounded quite cheerful, and not at all steady. "I'm just a large boy in a field. Enjoy it. Thirty-eight."

"Thirty-eight," said Kiva. "Thirty-nine."

She put her head down and tinned. It was the hardest thing she had done all that month. Every part of her that had gone over a rail was shouting, and the still door was heaving under her knees with its whole weight, wanting to be good. She gave it a number. She pressed the third lid and listened for it.

The husk came out of the soft earth onto the cinders, and it did not slow, and it did not see him. It walked into Perrin Cade exactly as it would have walked into a gatepost.

He had no stance. He had only himself. It took him in the hip and the ribs with its shoulder, and he went sideways off his feet with a grunt like a struck drum, into the bean poles, which went down in a long clattering row.

But a gatepost bends a line. The husk's blind front slewed a pace to the south as it shouldered through him. One thick grey limb came down on the corner weight of the cloth, a hand's width from the gathered ash, and the next came down on cinder, and it was past.

"Forty-four. Seal check!"

"Sound," said Anwen. Her voice was shaking. Her thumb was not.

"Anwen. Take the left pan. My hand's too slow, I'll lose it."

Anwen took the left pan. They brought the last of it up between them, out of the folded canvas, grey and warm and still listening, and Kiva ran the lazy finger round the rim and wound the cord and pulled it through.

"Fifty-one. Sealed."

At the east corner of the hold, a short red blade lit and fell, once. The second husk came apart against the silver wall. A grey-haired woman was on her knees beneath it with a cloth already spread, and Kiva, who had thought the warden quick, watched two pans move like a card trick.

"Fifty-nine," said Kiva. "Sixty."

The listening stopped.

She put the pan down. Her hands would not hold it any longer.

---

Perrin was sitting up among the bean poles with one hand pressed to his side.

"I'm alive," he said, before anyone could ask. "I want it noted that I was told to count and I counted. I lost my place at forty. I think it was the flying." He drew a careful breath and winced. "That's a rib. Maybe two. It didn't even look at me, Kiva. I've never been so thoroughly ignored."

Anwen was beside him before Kiva could get her legs under her. She laid her palms an inch above his ribs and shut her eyes, and her face went still and inward, the way it had gone over a sandbag in the dark. "Two," she said. "Cracked, not broken through. Nothing underneath. Your hands have split again." She opened her eyes. "You great fool."

"I'd like it said that I'm a great fool who got told first."

"It's said."

Kellan had come down the path. He stood at the edge of the cloth and looked at the four sealed tins in the carrier, and at the bare canvas, and then at the print of one thick foot on its corner weight.

"I'd have lit," he said.

"I know," said Kiva.

"I had my hand up. I'd decided." He was not looking at her. "It would have been a clean kill. It would have been the cleanest thing I did all day, and it would have printed the lot, or blown it into the next plot." He stopped. "You said *count*."

"It was all I could think of."

"It worked." He said it as if it annoyed him, and then, more slowly, as if it did not. "I heard a number and my mouth said the next one. I didn't know it would do that."

The white tape lifted. Hollis Vane ducked under it and came across the plot with his slate, and behind him came the grey-haired woman with a brass balance in a leather case exactly like Orla's.

"Bettan weighs on the bank," said Vane. "The graders will have their say tomorrow, when your tins have had a night in the cold. I like to know what I'm signing for today."

The woman called Bettan knelt and set the tins on the pan one by one. She did not hurry. She moved the little weights with a fingernail.

"Twenty," she said, "and eight-tenths."

Nobody spoke. Anwen's hand had closed on Kiva's sleeve.

"The mark's twenty," said Kellan, to no one.

Vane wrote. When he had finished, he turned the slate round and held it where the four of them could read it.

*T7. Abort 1, clean, 9 sec. Kill on 2nd entry. Catch sealed at 51. Nothing fouled. One hurt: Cade, ribs, by own choice, dark. Yield 20.8 on the bank, to grade.*

*Passed, subject to the tins.*

"I've four things to say, and then I've a tear to shut," he said. "Cade. That was a stupid thing to do, and it's the only way that cloth was getting saved, and you're the first Carry I've had all year who looked after the one member of his team nobody tells him to. Get those ribs strapped. Pryce. You called *off* in nine seconds on a thing you'd planned for, and you held a skinned Handful's face for a slow count of four without stepping back. Mace has told me who you are. I'll tell him what I saw."

Anwen went scarlet to the hair.

"Renn." Vane took his time. "You're the best blade that has come through this gap today, and I've had fourteen. You sat down when you were told. You waited for your foot. And at the end you were one breath from fouling your own team's catch to show me a kill I never asked for, and a provisional Third on her knees said *no*, and you listened." His pale eyes were level. "Which of those do you suppose I'm going to write down?"

Kellan met them.

"All of it," he said.

"All of it. Good. I wouldn't have you otherwise." He turned to Kiva last. "Who taught you to go dark?"

"Orla Dane. And the warden. And him." She nodded at Kellan. "He hit me with a blade for a week."

"Then they did it properly." He glanced at the tins. "You called that floor from your knees with a live one walking at you. I've known chartered catchers who couldn't. What I can't make out is the tin record on your certificate. Six drams of dead ash and somebody swinging at you. Whose notion was that?"

"Hers. Orla's."

"Hm." He chalked something small in the corner of the slate. "I might steal it."

"There's one more thing." Kiva had the pencil in her hand already, for the tags, and she was afraid that if she did not say it now she would talk herself out of it. "The skin. When it broke. Some of that ash had something in it before I touched it, and nobody was lit, I looked. It's in all four tins, a little. It isn't a print." She made herself finish. "The warden had me say it three times and wrote it in the Kennel book. It feels curved."

Hollis Vane did not say *hm*. He looked at her for rather a long time.

"Bettan," he said.

"I heard." The grey-haired woman had stopped with the balance half folded. "I had one off the Ferren hold, the fourteenth. With a skin. I thought my hands were tired."

Vane took the first of Kiva's tags out of her fingers. He turned it over, and on the back, in his quick slanting hand, he wrote a line and initialed it.

*Catcher reports skin ash "curved" inside the count, no lit door near. Second such report this month. H.V.*

"Put that one on top," he said, and gave it back.

---

The closing crew came down from the Brickfields road at half past five, six people with a handcart, and Hollis Vane went to meet them.

Kiva sat on the bank of the cut with her boots off and her feet in the cold brown water. She had written the four tags. *Sag Line, plot 19. The 28th. Team Seven. One Handful, skinned. Sealed at 16, 27, 44, 51.* And on each, on the line where the form asked for it, a thing she had never before had cause to write.

*Catcher: K. Rowan.*

It was only a paper tag on a tin. It would go in a cold room overnight, and tomorrow a grader would cut the cord and throw it in a basket. But it was the first time her name had been written on anything in the Compact without *unclassified* or *provisional* or *failed* somewhere near it.

Mara came down the berm steps with her tool roll under her arm.

She did not say that she had watched. She had told Kiva, on a tram stop a fortnight ago, that she would never be able to sit in a gallery, and Kiva had believed her. But the mast stood at the top of the berm with the whole of plot nineteen laid out below it like a map, and Mara had been at the foot of that mast with a drum roll for eleven hours, and there was cinder dust on her knees where she had knelt, at some point, at the edge of the road.

She sat down on the bank. After a moment she unlaced her own boots.

"You said *dark*," she said.

"Twice."

"I heard it from up there. Both times." Mara put her feet in the water beside Kiva's and hissed through her teeth at the cold. "I heard that boy say he was going to stand in front of you. I thought, *that's Cade's lad, he'll plant, he'll ruin it.* And then he didn't." She was quiet. "I nearly came down the bank. I had one foot over the edge of the road."

"What stopped you?"

"You were counting." Mara looked at the brown water. "I could hear you. Thirty-eight, thirty-nine. And I thought, if she can count, she doesn't need me shouting about quench handles."

Kiva leaned against her. She had not done that since she was small. Her aunt's shoulder was thin and hard and smelled of machine oil, and it did not move.

On the far side of the white tape, the six people from Brickfields had made a ring around the tear. She could not see what they did. She felt it, a slow tightening in the back of her neck, as if the thumb that had rested there all day were being lifted, a little at a time.

Agnes Roake was standing among her fallen bean poles with her hands on her hips. Perrin, strapped round the middle with somebody's scarf, was trying to stand one of them up again with his elbows.

"Leave it, you daft lump," Kiva heard her say. "You'll do yourself a mischief. I've a nephew."

"I knocked them down. I'd like to put them up. It's the only thing I've knocked down all month that I can."

"Then hold that one straight and stop talking."

The blue over the potato bed went grey, and then it was only air.

Kiva put her hand to her chest, out of habit. Through her shirt the disk lay where it always lay. It was cold. She found that she had not expected it to be anything else, and that today, for once, the cold did not feel like a verdict.

She had opened nothing at all. She had four tins.

---

## CHAPTER — books/book-2680-opening/manuscript/chapter-14.md

# Chapter 14 — Kiva: A Handful, Less a Pinch

The Wren House graded ash in a long north-facing room with no lamps in it.

Kiva had not expected that. She stood in the doorway on Monday afternoon with the rest of Team Seven and looked down a row of tall windows and a row of slate benches, and it took her a moment to understand what was missing. There was not a cell in the place. There was no lamp, no meter with a lit dial, no humming box on any wall. The scales were brass and the light was daylight.

"Nothing worked within thirty paces," said Kellan quietly. "It's the same on the Halden floor. My mother's bench has nine cups. This one's got seven."

He was looking at the room the way Perrin looked at a meat bun. He had spoken very little since the Sag Line. But he had been at the Wren House gate a quarter of an hour before any of them, and when the grader came down the row with their carrier in her hands, he stood up straight, as he would for a master.

She was a brisk, freckled woman called Halloran, with a jeweler's glass on a cord round her neck and her sleeves pinned back to the elbow. She set the four tins on the slate, glanced at the tags, and stopped at the top one. She turned it over and read the back.

"Which of you is the catcher?"

"I am."

"Hm." Halloran looked at her a moment longer than was comfortable. "We'll come to that. First things first. They've had their night. Put a hand on them, if you like. I always let a team do that."

Kiva laid her palm on each lid in turn. They were cold, as cold as the slate, and through the steel she felt nothing at all, no heat and no hum and no thread. It was only ash, settled and quiet and nobody's.

She had not known how tightly she was braced until it let go.

"Not printed," said Halloran. "No. I'd have had you in the salt room at eight if they were. Now. There are three things I'm paid to tell you, and people are forever mixing them up, so I'll do them one at a time."

She cut the cords. She emptied the four tins, one after another, into a wide brass pan on the long arm of the scale, and the ash went in like grey water. She slid the weight along the beam with one finger until it hung level.

"Yield. That's how much. Twenty drams and eight-tenths, and your Home Line weigher on the bank was dead right, as they generally are. The book gives a Handful twenty-four, so you left three and a bit in a potato bed." She wrote it on a card. "Over the mark."

"Next?" said Kellan.

"Grade. That's how clean, and how steady." She took a pinch from the pan and dropped it into one of the seven small cups, which sat in a ring of bare copper wire. Then she laid two fingers on the wire, and Kiva felt the hand door stir, far off, and lie down again. It was the smallest tune she had ever felt anyone lay, a single plain shape no bigger than a thimble. "I put a test pattern on it and let go, and I count how long it holds true before it starts to wander. First grade holds past a hundred. Second past sixty. Under that it's Third, and under thirty it's road-lamp ash." Her lips moved. The room was very still. "Seventy-nine. Eighty. Eighty-one, and there it goes. Second."

"What took it off First?" said Kellan. He asked it the way another boy might have asked the score of a match.

"Cinder. Look." She held out the jeweler's glass. Through it, the pinch in the cup was a field of soft grey boulders, and among them lay three or four hard black glittering ones. "You tinned a little of somebody's garden path along with your husk. It always happens out of doors. I've known chartered catchers who'd give a week's pay for a Second off a windy bank." She took the glass back. "A clean Second. I'd be pleased with it."

Perrin, who was strapped so tightly round the ribs that he had to turn his whole body to look at anything, said, "And the third thing?"

"The third thing is why I asked for the catcher."

---

She brought it from a cupboard, in a wooden case lined with green cloth.

It was a brass frame about the length of Kiva's forearm, and across it were strung nine reeds of thin bright metal, each a little shorter than the last, like the teeth of a comb. Under the reeds lay a narrow plate of polished steel.

"The comb," said Halloran. "Resonance. This is the one nobody outside this building understands, so listen." She set it on the slate. "Every husk was shaped by something. I don't mean somebody. I mean that whatever pressed it had a lean to it, and sometimes a little of that lean stays in the ash after the shape's gone. Each of these reeds is cut to answer one family of pattern. An edge. A stride. A ward. A healer's read. A stance. Four others you won't have met." She ran her thumbnail lightly down them, and they chimed in a falling scale. "Put ash on the plate, and if it has a lean, the reed that matches it will sing. We write that down as the resonance. It tells a tuner which kinds of work that ash will take to, and which it'll fight."

"And a Handful?" said Anwen.

"A Handful doesn't sing. That's what *plain* means. Handful ash is plain, and has been for as long as there's been a comb to put it on, which is three hundred years and some. A husk isn't enough of anything to have a lean. You start to get a note at Flask, sometimes. At Barrel, always." Halloran took a pinch from the brass pan. "That's why a big yield from an odd spawn is worth more than its weight. It isn't more fuel. It's fuel that already wants to do one particular job."

She let the pinch fall on the steel plate.

For a moment there was nothing. Then, very faintly, the third reed from the long end began to hum.

It was hardly a sound at all. Kiva would not have heard it in the shop, over the tick of the test rack. In that silent lampless room it was as clear as a fly on a windowpane, one thin steady silver note, and she knew it. She had heard it on a cinder path the day before, and in a brick hall the day before that. It was the note of a wet finger on the rim of a glass.

"Ward," said Halloran. "A trace of it. Not a tenth of what I'd get from a skinned Flask, but it's there, and it's not a print, because a print reads as one person and this doesn't read as anyone." She was watching Kiva. "You wrote *curved*. On the back of a tag, in a field, with no comb. The warden sent me a note on Saturday to say you'd told him the same thing on his floor, three times running, in the same words." She lifted the plate, and the reed fell silent. "I've been a grader nineteen years. I can't feel that through a tin. How did you know?"

Kiva did not know how to answer. Everyone was looking at her.

"I've always been able to feel what's in a cell," she said. "Without a door. It's just my hands. My aunt can do it too, a bit. In the count, the ash is all open, and it's like a room full of people waiting. And some of this wasn't waiting. It was already leaning on something." She felt her ears go hot. "I didn't know it was a ward. I only knew it was the shape Anwen's makes, when she holds it properly."

"Hm," said Halloran. She wrote on the card, three letters and a word. *W, trace.* "Come and see my shelf."

---

The shelf was at the far end of the room, between two windows.

It ran from floor to ceiling, and it held hundreds of small stoppered jars, each with a pinch of grey in the bottom and a card tied round its neck. They were sorted by yield and then by note. Kiva read the brass labels going up. *FLASK, EDGE. FLASK, WARD. BARREL, STRIDE.* Some of the cards were brown with age.

The lowest shelf of all was labeled *HANDFUL, PLAIN*, and it held a single enormous jar, the size of a bread crock, with one card on it that said only *As ever.*

Beside that crock, somebody had cleared a hand's width of bare wood and propped up a new label. It was not brass. It was a piece of white card, lettered in ink.

*HANDFUL, W-TRACE. First entered the 5th of this month.*

Four small jars stood behind it.

"Kennel, the fourth," said Halloran, touching them in turn. "Kennel, the twelfth. Kennel, the seventeenth. Kennel, Saturday. That last one's yours as well, off the warden's floor, and I did it this morning." She folded her arms. "Four in a month, from one lattice that's given plain husks since my grandmother was a girl. The board's had my note for three weeks. Do you know what they wrote back?"

"That it's the lattice," said Kellan.

She looked at him sharply.

"It's what I'd write, if I were sitting in an office and wanted it to be nothing," he said. "One tear. One sixty-year-old pattern holding it. If the pattern's begun to drift, every husk out of it would carry the drift. It's the cheapest explanation, and you can't disprove it with four jars from the same place."

"No," said Halloran. "I can't." She nodded at the brass pan on the scale, at the end of the long room. "But that didn't come out of the Kennel. That came out of a hole in the air over an allotment, three miles from any lattice. It's the first field Handful I've ever heard sing." She did not soften it. "I'd like half a dram for this shelf. I can't require it. It comes out of your team share, not the city's, and I know what a first share is. But a jar on this shelf with a Home Line officer's initials on its tag is something the board will have to answer. A note from me is something the board can file."

Nobody said anything. Half a dram did not sound like much. Kiva had spilled more than that off the edge of a cloth without noticing.

"We'll tell you tomorrow," said Anwen. "When we've seen what the share is."

"That's fair." Halloran took the card from the scale and handed it to Kellan, since his was the hand that had come out for it. "Rowan. Across the passage, third door. Healer Aske wants a look at two fingers, and said to send you over while the cups were running. Go on. They'll wait."

Healer Aske was small and brisk and had the coldest thumbs Kiva had ever met.

The examination took four minutes, and most of it was pressing. First finger, second, third. A pin, lightly. A feather. "Eyes shut. Which am I touching? And now?"

"First. No. Second."

"Mm." Aske let go of her hand. "They're slow. They aren't stopped. The first is very nearly true, and the second's a week or so behind it, and both of them are reporting late, like a clerk who's got the right answer and takes his time bringing it. Another month, I'd say, and you'll have to think about it to notice." The cold thumbs pressed once more, hard, at the base of the two fingers. "I'm told you caught a Handful with them."

"With the third one, mostly."

"Sensible. Keep doing that. And listen to me." Aske's eyes were grey and very direct. "That nerve has been burned once along the whole of its length. It's forgiven you. It won't do it twice. If there's a next time, you come here the same hour, not the next day, and I'll want ward-trace ash for the easing, because nothing else takes to a nerve half so kindly." There was a short dry laugh. "Which until this month I'd have had to beg from the Flask shelf, a pinch at a time. And now the catcher herself walks in with four drams of it. The world has a sense of humor. I've never much cared for it."

---

The Compact ash office was in the basement of the Measure Hall, behind a door as thick as her arm.

On Tuesday at noon a clerk with ink to the wrists brought out a grey ledger and a slate, and on the slate he had already done the sum. He turned it to face them across the counter and went through it with the end of his pen, briskly, as he must have done forty times that month.

"Yield at grade, twenty and eight-tenths, Second, W-trace. Civic power takes the half. Ten and four." He ticked it. "Of what's left, the casualty reserve takes the quarter. Two and six. Of what's left after that, the schools take the half, by head. Three and nine, of which Crooked Line has two heads and Stonehand and the Wren House one apiece." Another tick. "The remainder is the team's."

He underlined it.

*Team Seven: 3.9 drams.*

"Is that all?" said Perrin.

It came out before he could stop it. He went red to the ears.

"That's all," said the clerk, not unkindly. "It's a Handful, son. Less the city, less the hurt, less the schools. You'll find it goes up quicker than you'd think. A Flask is twenty of those." He opened the ledger. The first page had been ruled and headed, and under the heading were four names in a copperplate hand. *TEAM SEVEN. YIELD BOOK. Renn, K. Pryce, A. Cade, P. Rowan, K. (prov.)* "Would you like to see it?"

He fetched it from the strongroom. It was a squat tin like Druce's, but only half the size, with the Compact's seal pressed into the lid and a paper band round it. He broke the band and lifted the lid, and the four of them leaned over the counter.

Three drams and nine-tenths of ash lay in the bottom. It would have fitted in an egg cup.

Kiva looked at it for a long time. She thought of forty minutes on her knees in a brick hall, and a tin that opened when a man turned it over, and the weight of a dark blade on her ribs seven nights running. She thought of Perrin going sideways into the bean poles. It did not look like any of that. It looked like what you swept out from behind a stove.

She found that she did not mind. It was the first ash she had ever touched in her life that she had not been afraid of.

"Spend, save, or return," said the clerk. He closed the lid. "It isn't yours to carry about. It's the team's, on the book, and any entry wants all four signatures. There's a list on the wall of what a dram will buy. Take your time. I've had teams stand in that passage and shout at each other till four."

---

They did not shout. They did it at the Rowan kitchen table that evening, because it was the only table any of them had that was not in a laundry or a tram yard.

Mara gave them the lamp and a pot of tea and went down to the shop. "I'd have opinions," she said on the stairs. "And it isn't mine."

So there were four of them and a teapot, and in the middle of the table Anwen's copy of the wall list, in her small neat hand.

*Kennel floor, one release: 0.7.*
*Catch kit, new, school store: 1.0.*
*Meet reserve: up to 0.5 per fighter, above the standard dram, any sanctioned lane.*
*Healer's easing, per sitting: 1.0.*
*Travel warrant, regional qualifier: 2.0 a head.*
*Return to the reference shelf or to civic stock: at par, credited to the team's name.*

"I'll go first," said Kellan. "I want floors. All of it, if you'd let me, which you won't. Five releases and a bit." He had the grader's card in front of him, squared to the edge of the table. "We've cut a skin cleanly once. Once is an accident. The Flask qualifier is whenever the board says, and a skinned Flask will not let me be half a breath early. I want to have done it right three times before it's a thing that can kill Pryce."

"That's a reason," said Anwen. "It's not the only one."

"No." He did not pretend otherwise. "First Division's live lanes open next week. I'm behind every blade in my year by exactly the length of time I spent at the bottom of a call. Floors are how I get that back. It's for me as well as for us. I'd rather you knew which part was which."

"Kit," said Perrin.

He had been turning his cup round and round on the table.

"I know what you'll say. I know it was me. I brought a cloth with two corners and a box of table salt to a live floor, and if Anwen hadn't had a flask, we'd have had nothing to kill a print with. I've written it down. It's the worst page in my book." He put the cup down. "But I only brought that kit because we haven't got one. We've borrowed the warden's, and we've borrowed my Hall's, and next time we'll be borrowing somebody else's with something else wrong with it. I want a cloth that's ours. I want tins I've turned over myself." He breathed in, carefully, around the strapping. "And I want it said that when the man showed us that tin today, I thought, *is that all*, and I was ashamed, and I still think it. It's the first thing I ever earned that wasn't a number on somebody else's board. I'd like to be able to pick some of it up."

Nobody laughed. Kiva saw Kellan look at him with something that was almost recognition.

"Save," said Anwen. "At least a dram. Sealed, on the book, and nobody touches it." She had her list beside the other one, and for once she was not looking at either. "I stood at the tape of a Home Line hold all day on Sunday, watching. Doran Mace told me a thing there that I've not been able to put down. He said a new crew that spends its first share is the crew that can't afford its second mistake. Something will go wrong. It did on Sunday, and all it cost us was two of Perrin's ribs, because it was a Handful." Her level eyes went to Kiva's left hand, lying on the table. "Healer Aske said *next time, the same hour, and I'll want ward-trace.* I was in the passage. I heard. We've got ward-trace. I think we'd be mad to burn it on floors."

"Not for my fingers," said Kiva.

"For whoever's fingers. Yours are just the ones we know about."

"Then my interest's the same as Kellan's, turned round," Anwen went on, before Kiva could answer. "My placement wants a license, and I've got it now. What it wants next is for nobody on this team to be carried off a line this season with my name on the card. I'm not being generous. I'm being careful with something that's mine."

They all looked at Kiva.

She had known all day what she wanted, and it was not on the wall list in the order she wanted it.

"The shelf," she said. "The half dram she asked for."

"That's the one thing on there that buys us nothing," said Perrin.

"I know."

"I'd have put it first," said Kellan.

Kiva turned to him. So did the others.

"I was going to say so when it came round to me again." He was frowning at the grader's card. "When that supervisor changed the stamp on my mother's batch, the book saved her. I told you that. The book said what she'd measured. But the book was only her word, written down, until they went to the shelf. Every batch she'd ever flagged, she'd put a pinch of it in a jar, with the date, because that's the rule and she keeps rules. They took the jar down and measured it again, in front of everybody, eleven months later, and it read what she'd said it read." He put one finger on the card. "A number somebody can check is worth ten you have to be believed about. That jar is the only thing on this table that's still worth something if we turn out to be wrong about all the rest."

"So that's two for the shelf," said Anwen slowly, "from the two people at this table I'd have bet on to argue for floors and for herself."

"I'm not arguing against floors," said Kellan. "I'm saying which half dram I'd cut first, and it isn't that one."

Anwen picked up her pencil.

It took them the rest of the pot. In the end it was Perrin who did the sum, on the back of his hand, because he said he wanted to be the one who made it come out even.

"Half to the shelf. That's the jar. One dram sealed, and it stays ward-trace, and nobody so much as breathes on it unless a healer says so." He counted on his green and yellow fingers. "One for a kit. A whole one. With four corners. That's two and a half. Which leaves one and four-tenths, and at seven-tenths a go, that's two floors for Kellan to practice not being early." He looked round the table. "Three and nine. Nothing over. It comes out exact, which I take as a sign."

"There's nothing for a meet reserve," said Anwen. She was looking at Kiva. "Yours is Saturday. The list says half a dram. Your draw—"

"I know what my draw is."

"You'll run dry. In a team lane, three rounds, on one standard dram. You ran through nearly a whole one in a single bout in the Ring."

"I know." Kiva had turned this over on the tram, all the way home. "And if I do, I ought to find that out on the board, where it gets written down. Not have you three buy me out of it with ash you bled for. Saturday isn't Team Seven's. It's the Crooked Line third team's, and mine." She made herself say the rest. "If I need more ash than everyone else on that sand just to stay standing, then that's a true thing about me, and I'd rather know it than hide it."

Perrin gave a long sigh.

"Thank goodness," he said. "Because it's Stonehand you're fighting. My Hall. My own third team. And if you'd asked, I'd have had to sit here and vote to buy you ash to go and beat them with, and I would have done it, Kiva, I'd have put my name to it. And then I'd have complained every day for a month, to all of you, at length."

"You'll complain anyway," said Kellan.

"I shall. But now it'll be about ribs."

They signed it on Wednesday morning, at the counter, in the grey ledger. Four names, one under another. The clerk blotted them and wrote the entries out beneath in his copperplate, and Kiva watched three drams and nine-tenths of ash turn into four lines of ink.

It did not feel like spending. It felt like ruling columns in an empty book.

---

Noa Bexley had been in the roster corridor since the Measure Hall opened, and he had not yet looked at a single roster.

The frames went back two hundred years along both walls. Everybody who came up here looked at those. What nobody looked at was the row of long flat drawers beneath them, where the Placement Chair's office kept the Classification Day summary sheets, one to a year, each a single ruled page with the whole of an entering class boiled down to a dozen figures. Any rostered student might read them. You signed a card. Noa's was the only signature on the card since the autumn before last.

He had forty of them spread along the floor in order, weighted at the corners with his boots.

He had come to check one thing. He was a person who checked things, which was why he was Third Division with an Ember rank he had not bothered to retest in a year. Other people in the Low Lane fought. Noa fought when he had to, quite fast and not very well, and the rest of the time he kept the tapes, because a tape was the only thing in the whole Circuit that told you what had happened rather than who had won.

The one thing was the bench standard. If the draw bench had been wrong about Kiva Rowan by a factor of three, he wanted to know whether it had been drifting for everyone. A sticky needle. A tired spring. She had said something of the kind herself, on Classification Day, to a clerk who had not written it down.

So he had pulled the class median for Draw, year by year, and it had not drifted at all. One point nought. One point nought one. Nought point nine nine. Forty years of it, as flat as a millpond.

It was the next column over that had stopped him.

*Median Hold.* Five and three-tenths. Five and four. Five and three. Five and five, in a year the clerk had marked *strong class*. It went on like that down the whole length of the corridor, a long quiet line of fives, and he would never have looked twice.

The sheet under his left boot was this spring's. *Median Hold: 6.1.*

He had checked the arithmetic. Then he had gone back to the classification ledger itself and done the median again by hand from three hundred and six names, because a clerk could slip, and it had come out six and one.

He read down the rest of that last sheet with his pencil. Fire readings at seventeen: three. He ran back along the floor. In the ten years before, taken all together, there had been two. Lowest Draw in the class: nought point seven, a girl from the Weighbridge. He had to go back to the sheet for 2651 to find a better. And down at the foot, in the space where the Chair's office noted anything out of the way, somebody had written in a long plain hand: *Stonehand reports eleven candidates at or above upper Flame. Usual: three or four. Confirmed on re-read.*

Noa sat back on his heels.

A strong year. That was what anyone would say, and they would be right, in the sense that it was a year and it was strong. Jude would say it and go back to chalking the litter poles. It was probably nothing. A class was three hundred people. Three hundred people could throw up a freak median the way a handful of dice could throw up five sixes.

He took the notebook out of his coat. It was a cheap one, ruled in his own columns, and near the back there was a page he had headed, some time in the winter, *Things that are probably nothing.*

There were six lines on it. The fourth, dated the twenty-second, said *Two tears in nine days. Usual, 4–5 a spring.* The fifth, from yesterday, was copied off a notice the Kennel warden had pinned to the Cellhouse gate: *Skinned Handfuls: four on the Kennel floor this month, one in the field. None prior, in sixty years of book.* The sixth was a number from the Low Lane mast with a question mark after it.

He wrote a seventh. *Class of 2680: median Hold 6.1 against forty years at 5.3–5.5. Three Fire at seventeen. Chair's office has noticed.*

He looked at the page. They were not the same sort of thing at all. Two were about husks and one was about students and one was about a girl's draw, and the only connection between them was that they had all turned up in the same notebook in the same month. He was not fool enough to think that meant anything. He drew no line between them.

He did turn the page, though, to the other one, the one he had not shown to Jude.

It had her name at the top. Under it he had ruled five columns. *Door. Output. Draw. Recovery. Behavior.* There was one row filled in, from the litter, and one from the Terrace Ring tape, and a great deal of blank paper.

He wanted to fill it. He wanted it the way other people in his year wanted a First Division band. He had never in his life been near a question that nobody else had asked yet, and this one had walked into the Low Lane with her arm in a sling and sat down under the next peg. If it was what he thought, there would be a paper in it. His name would be on the paper. He was honest enough to know that he wanted that very much, and that wanting it was not the same as being owed it.

Because they were her numbers. That was the difficulty, and it was inconvenient. He could pull her tape every night and nobody would stop him. The Chair had ordered it pulled. He could fill those five columns without asking her one thing, and take them up these stairs to that long plain handwriting, and she would find out when somebody sent for her.

He had seen how the school looked at her. A quench box in front of a peg. He did not much want to be one more person who had decided what Kiva Rowan was without mentioning it to her.

Noa gathered up the forty sheets, in order, and put them back in their drawers. On the card he signed his name beneath his name.

He would ask on Saturday. After the meet, when there would be a sanctioned draw board with a referee's stamp on it, and not just a sixty-year-old mast in a cart shed. He would say what he wanted and why, and that he would like his name on it second, after hers. And if she said no, he would go on measuring, because he could not help that, and file nothing.

He thought she would probably say yes. He wrote that down too, under *probably nothing*, and then crossed it out, because it was not evidence.

---

"It's a response," said Jude.

He had chalked the Long Terrace on the Low Lane's brick floor, a rectangle forty paces by fourteen shrunk to fit, with a line across the middle and a box at each end. He stood on the line with a litter pole in his hand, like a pointer.

"That's how we'll play it, because it's the only way any of us has ever worked as four. You've all had a month on the warden's floor. So. Stonehand comes out of that box, four still-doors, in a row. They don't run. They've never run. They walk to the litter like a dray and pick it up and walk it home, and anything in the way gets leaned on." He tapped the middle line. "Noa's quicker than all four of them put together. He gets to Donny first and drags him our way. Three paces, four, whatever he can. That's the Carry."

"I can't lift him alone," said Noa. "Struts sink under a load. I can drag."

"Drag, then. Pru. Ward across the middle, here, the moment they're close. That's the Hold. You're keeping them where we want them."

"They'll walk through it," said Pru. "I had one of them walk through it last year. He said *excuse me*."

"They'll walk into it. That's a second and a half they're not walking into Noa. I take the touches." He put the pole to his own chest. "Every one of them that reaches for a handle has to open up to do it. That's my Kill. A point a time, and ten counts on their knees for each." He looked at Kiva. "And you're on the litter. Still door, both hands on the poles, planted on top of him like a hen. They can't carry what they can't lift. I want you to be the heaviest thing on that sand."

It was a good plan. She could see every part of it. It had four jobs in it, and each of them had been hers or her friends' on a brick floor that same month.

"Still," she said. "That's what I'll declare."

"And if you have to change?"

"I say so first. Out loud. To the referee. *Rowan, changing,* and the door." She had practiced the words in front of the lane warden until he had asked her, wearily, to stop. "It takes me about two seconds from the one to the other, clean. I can't do it faster yet."

Jude rubbed his chipped tooth.

"Two seconds is a long time on sand," he said. "Try not to need it."

She spent most of Thursday with Pru Finch, at the far end of the lane, because Pru's ward came up flat.

It was a good ward, bigger than Kiva's own and a great deal steadier. But Pru held it out before her at the length of both arms, like a woman carrying a tray of glasses across a crowded room, and when Jude leaned his shoulder into the edge of it, it rang twice and went with a splash of cold air.

"That's what he did," said Pru. "The Stonehand one. Only he was bigger."

"You're standing behind it," said Kiva. "Try standing under it."

She was not the right person to teach this. She said so. Her own ward was the size of a tea tray and her Hold on it was two and six-tenths. But she had knelt a forearm's length from Anwen Pryce on a cinder path while Anwen held a skinned husk's whole weight on a ward for a slow count of four, and she had watched every inch of how Anwen did it, because there had been nothing else in the world to look at.

"Hands low. Lower. Wide, like you're pushing a cart. And don't hold it up. Lean on it. It isn't a thing you're carrying. It's a door, and you're keeping it shut with your shoulder."

Pru tried it. The ward came up crooked, wobbled, and curved.

Jude leaned on it. It rang, and rang, and rang again. He had to step round the end of it in the finish.

"Oh," said Pru faintly. "Oh, that's *horrible*. It's so much easier. I've done it wrong for three years. Who told you that?"

"A friend," said Kiva. "She told me I splash. I still do."

---

Perrin was waiting at the Stonehand gate on Friday when she brought the Hall's old kit back.

He took it from her with his elbows, since his hands were bound again. Then he held up, between two wrapped fingers, a brown paper parcel tied with string.

"Ours," he said. "I went to your school's store this morning and stood in the queue with the team book. Open it. I can't."

She undid the string. Inside lay a waxed cloth, new and stiff and smelling of linseed, with a lead weight sewn fast into each corner. There were two pans without a dent in them, and a brush, and a wet-salt box with a rubber seal in the lid, and six squat tins with their cords coiled white inside.

"Four," said Perrin. "Corners. I counted them in the shop, and then I made the man count them." He was pink with pride. "And I've wet the salt. It's so wet it's nearly soup."

"It's lovely, Perrin."

"It is, isn't it?" He looked at it a moment longer, and then his face changed, and he glanced back over his shoulder at the long stone front of the Hall. "Listen. About tomorrow. I'm going to say one thing, and then I'm not going to say another word, and you're not to ask me."

"I wasn't going to ask."

"I know you weren't. That's why I'm saying one thing." He lowered his voice. "Their captain's Lowen Brask. Third-year. He ran the junior lanes when I came, and he's the one who taught me to say thank you to the ground. He's the best teacher in the Hall, and he's been Third for three years because his Hold stops at seven and a half and won't go higher, and he's stopped minding." Perrin swallowed. "He reads people. That's what he does instead. He'll read you like a tag on a tin. That's all. I've said too much, and it's my Hall, and I'm going to go and feel disloyal to two places at once."

He went, with the old kit under one arm, and did not look back.

At home, the Works notice had company beneath the brass weight. A second paper lay on top of it, a half sheet in a quick slanting hand that she knew at once.

*To whom it concerns at the Works. Mast six, Sag Line: left uncorrected by its instrument-keeper, with her reason written in its book. On the 28th it rang four minutes ahead of five and seven. Hold established with ninety seconds in hand. H. Vane, Home Line watch.*

"It came by the noon post," said Mara. She was mending a kitchen meter, with her glasses on and the tweezers in her left hand. "It isn't a license. It's a man's handwriting on half a sheet of paper." She did not look up. "I've known that to count for something. We'll see on the ninth."

That night, in the Old Yard, Orla sat on her crate with her knee bound in three thicknesses of flannel and read the week's pages.

She read them by the lamp, slowly, one finger moving down the columns. She read the Kennel and the cold room and the Sag Line. She read the kitchen table. At the end she closed the book and held it on her lap.

"Stonehand, tomorrow," she said.

"Yes."

"And your captain's built it like a floor."

"Kill, Hold, Carry, Catch. It's a good plan."

"It's a very good plan for a husk." Orla handed the book back. "I've fought people and I've fought spawn for forty years, and I could tell you tonight, in about six sentences, what the difference is going to do to you. You'd nod. You'd write it down. And tomorrow you'd do exactly what your hands have been taught these three weeks, because that's what hands do." She shook her head. "It'll keep better if you find it yourself. Go and lose it properly. Bring me the page."

"You think we'll lose."

"I think you've a sanctioned meet with a referee and a draw board, and one incident ends your season and my standing, and you're to declare every door before you use it." Her crooked face was not unkind. "Lose it by the rules, Kiva. That's all I'll ask. Now do me the drill once before I'm too stiff to count it."

She did the drill. She looked at the crooked post and not at the metal.

"Eight and six," said Orla.

---

## CHAPTER — books/book-2680-opening/manuscript/chapter-15.md

# Chapter 15 — Kiva: The Carry

Stonehand Hall did not believe in flat ground, and the Long Terrace was the proof of it.

It lay at the heart of the Hall, under a roof of glass and iron, and from the gallery rail it looked like a dry dock with the ship taken out. The court was sunk a full story below the benches. It ran forty paces from end to end and fourteen from side to side, and nearly every part of it had been built to trip somebody.

At each end stood a stone platform, two knee-high steps above the floor, which the Hall called a porch. A single low step ran the whole length of both long walls. The floor between was coarse, pale sand, raked into lines, and straight across the middle of it lay a curb of worn stone no higher than an ankle, which the Hall called the sill. On the sill, on its canvas litter, with its arms folded on its chest, lay a sandbag in a coverall.

"That's theirs," said Pru Finch, at Kiva's elbow. "They've got one called Old Tom. He's been carried over our line so many times they've had his name embroidered."

"Pru," said Jude.

"I'm only telling her. She ought to know who she's rescuing."

It was Saturday afternoon. The gallery was full to the roof, and the noise of it came down into the court like weather. Kiva stood on the western porch with the other three, in a grey Third Division tunic that was a size too large, and let herself look up once.

Mara was there. She had said nothing about coming. She had put on her good coat after dinner and walked to the tram, and now she was sitting in the fourth row beside Orla Dane, very upright, holding her gloves in her lap like a woman at a hearing. Orla's bound leg stuck out into the aisle. Two rows behind them sat Anwen, with her list, and Kellan, whose own lane was the last on the card and who had come three hours early to watch this one with a pencil.

Further along, where the chairs had arms, there were coats of slate blue. One of the people wearing them was a tall woman with a white streak through her dark hair. Kiva knew that streak. She had seen it once before, pressed close to the glass of another gallery, on the worst afternoon of her life.

She did not know why a Graywater officer should come to a Third Division lane. She put the question away with the others and brought her eyes back down.

"Fighters to the sill," said the referee.

---

It was the same referee. Kiva recognized the grey braid and the voice like a dropped crate, and by the look she got, the recognition was mutual.

The two teams met at the stone curb with Old Tom between them.

She had never seen four still-doors stand in a row before. They did not look like fighters. They looked like furniture that somebody had carried in and set down. The largest of them, a boy with a neck as thick as his head, was introduced as Hendry Moss, and beside him stood a long-armed, sharp-faced girl called Idony Keld, who was already looking at Kiva's left knee. The fourth was a first-year with ears like jug handles, who seemed more frightened than Pru.

Their captain put out his hand to Jude across the litter, and then, to her surprise, to her.

"Lowen Brask."

He was broad and unhurried, with a shaved head and very calm brown eyes, and his grip was careful. He had bare forearms and no cuff on either. None of the four wore an Ember.

"I watched your Cellhouse plate," he said. "Twice. You root better than half my second-years, and you'd had no teaching. I thought you ought to be told I'd looked." He let go of her hand. "It didn't seem right to know more about you than you knew I knew."

"Thank you," said Kiva, and found that she meant it, and that it frightened her rather more than if he had sneered.

"The Carry," said the referee. "For the benefit of the gallery and the two first-years. Three carries. Four minutes to each, by my glass. You begin on your own porch. The litter begins here. Put it on your own porch, the whole of it, and that's three points and the carry's done." She held up a thick hand and folded down the fingers. "A clean contact on the body or the head puts a fighter down. That's one point. A fighter who's down kneels where they are for a count of ten from the line judge. They don't touch the litter, and nobody touches them. A contact on a limb is a half, and two halves on one fighter in one carry make a down. Still-doors score with the open hand, or with a throw that puts both shoulders on the sand. Embers stay dark. Light an edge in my court and you'll be watching the rest from up there."

"And at time?" said Jude, who knew, but who had seen Kiva's face.

"If the glass runs out with the litter on the sand, it's one point to whichever half it lies in. Draw board's on the east wall. One standard dram each for the whole lane, three carries, and if you overspend it, that's a violation and a point off." Her eyes moved along the western four. "Doors."

"Talley. Ember."

"Finch. Hand."

"Bexley. Ember."

"Rowan. Still."

The referee did not write that one down straight away.

"I've a note about you from the Placement Chair," she said. "It says you'll tell me before you change. Is that right?"

"Yes. *Rowan, changing,* and then the door."

"Then here's how it goes in my court. You say it. I put up this flag." She showed a square of white cloth on a short stick. "You don't open the new one until the flag's up. Not when you've said it. When I've heard it. If you go before the flag, that's undeclared, and I'll score it so. I'm not having it argued afterward that you whispered."

It was fair. It was also a whole second longer.

She saw Lowen Brask hear it. He did not smile. He only looked at the white flag, and then at her, with the mild attention of a man reading the label on a jar.

---

The bell went, and Noa was off the porch before it had finished ringing.

He went down the two steps on his struts and across the sand in four long bounds, and Kiva saw the metal dig in at every stride and saw him ride it, light and crooked, like a boy running on a mattress. He had Old Tom by the foot poles while the four still-doors were still coming down their own steps.

They did not run. Jude had said so. They walked, shoulder to shoulder, with a slow rolling heavy tread, as if the four of them were carrying a piano that was not there.

"Drag!" said Jude. "Pru, the middle. Kiva, with me."

She could not Stride. She went down the steps on her own feet, minding the knee, and across fifteen paces of raked sand at something between a walk and a run. Noa had dragged the litter four paces into their half and was hauling at it with his heels dug in. She dropped to it. She took the two head poles in her fists and let her weight go down.

The still door opened like a cellar. The sand took her. She felt her knees go soft and sure, and the litter under her hands became the heaviest thing in the Hall.

"Planted," she said.

Pru's ward went up across the court behind her, wide and curved and leaning.

For about the space of ten breaths, it worked exactly as Jude had chalked it.

Moss reached the ward first, and leaned on it. It rang, and rang, and held, and Kiva heard Pru give a small astonished squeak. Keld did not lean on anything. She looked at the silver, and then she walked four paces to the right, to where it ended, and went round the end of it as a person goes round a puddle.

"She's just walked *round*," said Pru. "Jude, she's—"

"Hold Moss. I have her."

But he did not have her, because he was watching the other two. Brask and the boy with the ears had come straight on down the middle while the ward was busy, and they had arrived. The boy bent for the foot poles. Noa darted in and slapped his arm with a dark, stubby half-formed blade.

"Half! Crooked Line."

The boy flinched from it. As he flinched, his ribs came open, and Jude Talley, who had been waiting three years for exactly that, laid the flat of his dark edge across them as neatly as buttering bread.

"*Down!* Stonehand!"

And Kiva's hands let go.

They did not come off the poles. But for three weeks, on a brick floor and a cinder path and in the long hot hours at the kitchen table, a shape had gone down beside her, and a voice had shouted, and every part of her had done one thing. *Dark. Count.* The stance eased under her as if it had been greased. Her eyes went to the sand at her right, where a grey shape was folding to its knees, to see where it would fall.

It lasted one breath.

Lowen Brask was standing at the foot of the litter with a pole in each hand. He did not snatch. He simply straightened his back.

The poles slid through her slack fingers. Old Tom went up at the far end and came away from her, and by the time her weight dropped back into the sand, there was nothing in her fists but air, and the litter was a pace off and moving, its head end plowing a furrow.

"No," said Kiva.

She was rooted on nothing. The still door was wide open under her, holding up nobody, and Keld had come round and caught the trailing poles and lifted, and now two of them had it between them and were walking east.

She tried to turn. It was like turning a cart.

Jude had gone after them. He could not get past Keld's long arm. Noa was circling like a terrier. Nobody was going to stop that litter with a blade, and the only person on her side who could put a shoulder to it was planted fifteen paces behind with the wrong door open.

"Rowan, changing!" she shouted. "Ember!"

She saw the white flag go up. It seemed to take a week.

She gave the ground its weight back. *Thank you.* She shut the still door behind it and counted one, and reached for the slab, and the metal came off her cuff and ran across to her right fist and began to close—

She had been watching the flag. She had not been watching Idony Keld.

Keld had set her end of the litter down the moment the word *changing* left Kiva's mouth. She had covered six paces of sand in the time it took a referee to raise a stick. Her palm arrived, flat and hard and exactly on time, in the middle of Kiva's ribs, while the bar was still soft on her knuckles.

"*Down!* Crooked Line!"

Kiva knelt in the sand with the breath knocked out of her.

*One,* said the line judge. *Two.*

She counted with him. There was nothing else to do. From her knees she watched Jude put Hendry Moss down with a neat touch under the arm, and watched Moss kneel as placidly as a man sitting down to supper. She watched the boy with the ears finish his own ten and get up and trot to the litter. She watched Brask and Keld walk Old Tom up two stone steps without breaking stride, as people walk upstairs with the washing.

"*Carried!* Stonehand!"

The glass on the referee's table had more than a minute of sand left in it.

The board on the east wall turned over its figures. *STONEHAND 4. CROOKED LINE 2.* And beneath, in the column by her name: *Dram, 0.38.*

---

Perrin Cade had both wrapped hands clamped between his knees so that he would not do anything with them.

He was in the front row of the gallery, with the Hall's first-years, in the Hall's colors. He had sat there because it was where he belonged. His bursary had the Hall's seal on it. So did his tunic. The people around him had been stamping their feet since the word *carried*, and he had stamped twice, and stopped, and felt like a liar in both directions.

Brask had seen it. Perrin had watched him see it.

It was a thing Brask used to say in the junior lane, on wet mornings, to rows of thirteen-year-olds who were not listening. *Everybody's ready for a thing to start. Watch what they do when it stops.* He would shove a boy, and the boy would brace, and Brask would nod. Then he would clap his hands and say *good, that's all*, and shove him again as he relaxed, and the boy would sit down in the sand. Perrin had sat down in that sand perhaps forty times before he had learned.

Kiva had never been taught by Lowen Brask. She had been taught, for three weeks, by a brick floor, that when a thing went down beside her, the right and clever and disciplined answer was to go soft and look at the ground.

He had seen her do it on the Sag Line. He had thought it was the best thing he had ever watched.

Down on the western porch, the four of them had their heads together. Talley was chalking on the stone. Kiva had one hand flat against her ribs. From here she looked very small in that tunic.

On the eastern porch, Brask had not gathered anyone. He was sitting on the top step with his forearms on his knees, saying something short to Moss. Moss nodded. Then Brask's eyes went across forty paces of sand and rested on Kiva Rowan's hands.

*He's going to do it on purpose,* thought Perrin. *He's going to give her one.*

He could have shouted it. He had the lungs. It was not against any rule that he knew of, and a year ago he would not have thought twice.

But that was his captain down there, on his Hall's sand, who had taught him to thank the ground. And an hour ago, on the rack at the north end, in front of this same gallery, three first-years had put their names where Perrin's ought to have been.

He had not let himself look at the wall since. He looked now.

The brass plate was as long as a door. It carried the Hall's best first-year hold for every year since the rack was built, and the same line had headed it since long before Perrin was born. *340 kilos, thirty counts. 2649.* Thirty-one years. Masters had grown old under that number.

Today a girl from the Weighbridge had held three hundred and fifty. Then a boy Perrin shared a bench with had held three fifty-five. Then a third, a quiet lad nobody had thought much of, had stood under three hundred and sixty-two with his face perfectly calm, and the old master with the chisel had laughed out loud and said it was a strong year, and that he would need a longer plate.

Perrin had held four hundred. On his back, on his palms, for forty seconds, with a winch dead above him. Everyone in Meridian had seen it. But it had not been on the rack, and so it was not a hold, and he had been down to try for the plate today until a husk walked through him in a potato bed.

The Hall healer had looked at the strapping and laughed too, less kindly.

He had not told Kiva. He did not intend to. She would write it in that book of hers, under *what it cost*, and look at him with that awful steady face, and he would rather have the ribs.

*I'd do it again,* he thought, and it was true. *And I mind.* That was true as well. The two things sat side by side in his chest like a pair of badly matched boots, and he was getting used to the walk.

He kept his hands between his knees. He was Stonehand. He would be Stonehand tomorrow.

But when the bell went for the second carry, Perrin found that he was not watching the litter at all. He was watching Hendry Moss, who was coming down the eastern steps with his big arms hanging loose and open, like a man walking out to be measured for a coat.

---

"Rowan. Ember," she told the referee.

Jude had changed the chalk. It was simple enough. Stonehand walked through wards and round them, and they did not care about being touched. So the litter would not sit. It would move. Noa and Pru would lift it between them and carry it home, and Jude and Kiva would stand between the carriers and anything that came.

"The bar stops a hand as well as it stops a blade," Jude had said. "Kellan Renn couldn't get round it. Let's see if a girl with long arms can."

The bell went.

Noa was first again. Pru ran, which Kiva had never seen her do, with her scarf coming undone, and reached him as he got the foot poles up. They had Old Tom off the sill and swinging before the four still-doors were halfway. Pru's face was already crimson. It was twenty paces to the western porch.

Idony Keld came straight for the carriers, and Kiva stepped into her road.

The bar was on her fist, dark, forearm-long and ugly. Keld's palm came at her chest, and she brought the flat of the metal across, and the palm stopped dead against it with a smack like a butcher's slab.

Keld's eyebrows went up. She struck again, with the other hand, lower. The bar was there.

It was the best that Kiva had felt on that sand all day. *This* was what the thing was for. It could not cut and it could not reach, but it was as broad as a shovel, and behind it the litter was moving west, a pace and another pace, with Pru gasping and Noa shouting at her to mind the furrow.

Keld's eyes dropped to Kiva's left knee.

Her weight shifted. Her right hand swept in low and fast at the outside of that leg.

Kiva had seen a husk come straight at her forty times that month. Not one of them had ever started to go one way and gone another. She turned the bar down, and put her weight on the knee to do it, and the knee told her, with a flash straight up the thigh, what it thought of the idea. Keld's right hand had never been coming at all. Her left clipped Kiva smartly on the upper arm.

"Half! Stonehand."

"Kiva! Their captain!"

She looked round.

Lowen Brask had not gone to the litter. He had not gone to anyone. He had walked, at that same rolling unhurried pace, the whole length of the court, and now he stood at the foot of the western steps with his heels against the bottom riser.

He was planted. She could feel it from ten paces, a weight in the sand like a boulder under a lawn. And the litter, with Noa and Pru staggering at either end, was coming straight toward him, because that was where home was.

Jude went at him. The dark edge flicked at his ribs and found a forearm in the way.

"Half! Crooked Line."

It flicked again, and Brask turned his shoulder, and it slid off. He did not hit back. He did not need to. He only stood there, guarded, immovable, with the step at his back, and waited for the litter to walk into him.

"I can't get a clean one while he's set," said Jude. He was breathing hard. "Somebody's got to unset him. Or go through."

There was one person on the western side who could carry a litter into a planted still-door and not be stopped by it. She had done it twelve days ago, for four minutes, for nine hundredths of a dram.

She had the wrong door open.

"Rowan, changing!" It came out cracked. "Still!"

The flag. She did not watch the metal. She looked at Brask's shaved head and felt the copper take the weight, and breathed out, and counted one, and reached under her feet.

She was open for two seconds. Keld knew it. Kiva saw the long arms start toward her, and saw Jude step across and take the palm on his own bar, and thought, *he's covered me.* And then she thought, *and so nobody's on Brask.*

The still door came up. She took the head poles out of Pru's shaking hands.

"Go and ward," she said. "I've got him."

The weight went down into the sand. She walked.

It was the wading walk, a little taken and a little given back at every step, and her knee burned, and the litter came on behind her like a barge. Brask watched it come. When the front of the canvas was a pace from him, he put both broad hands flat on Old Tom's chest and leaned.

They stopped.

She had never felt another person's stance from the inside of her own. It was like putting a shoulder to a loaded wagon and discovering that somebody on the other side was doing the same. He was stronger than she was. She knew it in the first breath. Her hold was six and two on a good day and his was seven and a half every day, and the difference came up through the poles into her wrists as a slow, steady, patient pressure. She was not going forward. She was, by the width of a finger at a time, going back.

But she was holding. And he was held. His hands were on the litter and his weight was in it, and for as long as that lasted, he could not guard his ribs.

"Jude!" she said, through her teeth. "*Now*—"

Something large moved at the edge of her eye.

Hendry Moss had walked up beside her. He was close enough to touch. He was not looking at her, or at the litter, or at anything. His great arms hung at his sides, wide and loose, and his chest was as open as a barn door.

Jude's blade lay across it before he had finished arriving.

"*Down!* Stonehand!"

Moss knelt. He knelt at her right elbow, slowly, a big grey shape going down to the sand.

And Kiva went dark.

She knew what she was doing while she did it. That was the horror of it. She felt the stance ease. She felt her eyes pull down and to the right, to mark the fall, and she had time to think, quite clearly, *he's not ash*, and it made no difference at all. The hands had been taught. For one breath, the still door held nothing.

Lowen Brask pushed.

Seventy kilos of sand and canvas came back through her arms like a ram. The poles took her in the hips. Her heels went, and her bad knee folded, and the sky over the Long Terrace was made of glass and iron and was suddenly all she could see.

Both shoulders. She felt them hit.

"*Down!* Crooked Line!"

---

She lay on her back and listened to the count.

*One. Two.* Somewhere above her head, feet were moving on sand, heavy and unhurried. The litter went past. She saw the underside of it, and Old Tom's embroidered name, upside down.

*Five. Six.*

She had been given him. That was what she could not stop turning over. Moss had not been beaten. He had walked up and held his arms out. Brask had handed Jude a point, as you hand a dog a biscuit, so that a shape would go down at her elbow, because he had watched her in the first carry and had known to the half breath what she would do.

A husk did not do that. A husk came straight. A husk did not remember you from one minute to the next.

*Nine. Ten.*

She got up. The litter was thirty paces off, nearly at the eastern steps, with Brask at one end and the boy with the ears at the other. Jude and Noa were worrying at them. Keld was holding both of them off with her long arms, and her face was a little bored.

There was no still-door answer to thirty paces. There was nothing to plant on.

"Rowan, changing. Ember."

The flag. The gap. Nobody came for her this time. They did not have to.

She ran, on a knee that would not properly bend, with the bar on her fist. She reached the steps as Jude finally got a clean touch through Keld's guard, and the referee's voice said *down, Stonehand*, and it did not matter, because Old Tom was already lying on the eastern porch with his arms folded.

"*Carried!* Stonehand!"

The gallery roared. The board turned over.

*STONEHAND 8. CROOKED LINE 4.*

*Rowan. Dram, 0.87.*

---

Nobody on the western porch said anything for a while.

Pru sat on the top step with her head between her knees. Jude stood with his hands on his hips, looking at the board as a man looks at a bill. Kiva lowered herself onto the stone and stretched out her left leg, and under her tunic, the bottom heat-line ached, one long dull thread. Only the one. She had not lit two all afternoon. It seemed a very small thing to have managed.

"He gave us Moss," said Pru, into her knees. "He just *gave* him to us. Like a parcel."

"He's been giving us points since the first bell," said Jude. He rubbed the chipped tooth. "Four touches to their two. I thought we were winning the fights."

"We were," said Kiva. "It isn't a fight."

They looked at her.

She had not known what she was going to say until she had begun. But it had been laying itself out in her head since she lay on her back in the sand, piece by piece, as a fault lays itself out on the bench once you have the case off.

"It's what the Home Line officer said on Sunday. He doesn't keep a kill column. Brask doesn't either. He'll take a touch any time, if it buys him the litter. We've been playing it like a floor. Kill it, hold it, carry it." She pressed her hand to her ribs. "But a husk can't choose to lose a leg to get past you. He can."

"That's the plan," said Jude slowly. "That's my plan you're talking about."

"It's a good plan. I thought so too." She made herself go on. "The rest is me. Two things. When one of them goes down beside me, I go soft. I look at the ground. I've spent three weeks learning to do exactly that, and I can't seem to stop it. He saw it in the first carry, and in the second he made it happen." She held up a second finger. "And every time I change doors, I have to say so, and wait for a flag. That's two seconds where I've got nothing open, and I shout to the whole court when it's about to start. The first time, she hit me. The second time, you had to come off their captain to stop her. The third time, they didn't even bother."

"You're allowed three doors," said Pru. "I've only got the one. I'd have thought—"

"So did I," said Kiva. "For four years. I thought if they'd only let me use them, I'd be worth three people." It was oddly easy to say, sitting on a stone step with sand down the back of her neck. "I've used all three today. Every one of them was the right door for what was in front of me. And all it's given him is three different places to stand while I'm busy getting from one to the next."

Noa had not been listening. He had been at the east wall, at the foot of the draw board, where the mast's paper drum turned behind glass with eight pens on it. He came back along the side step at a trot with a length of tape pinched between his fingers.

"Look," he said. He knelt and spread it on the stone. "That's you. Fourth pen. I asked the line judge and he let me tear it. First carry. See how flat it is while you're planted? Hardly anything. Then here." His inky finger stopped at a sharp peak. "That's *changing*. And here it stays up, the whole time you've got the bar. Second carry. Up from the bell, because you started on Ember. Then a spike, down for forty seconds, another spike, up again." He looked up at her, and his narrow face was alight. "It's not you that's dear. It's the Ember door, and it's the getting there. On the still door, you spent two hundredths in forty seconds with their captain leaning on you. Two hundredths."

"How much have I got left?"

"Thirteen."

"For four minutes."

"On the bar, you'd be dry inside one. On the still door—" He did the sum with his lips. "You'd finish with something over. Not much."

Kiva looked across the sand. Brask was sitting on his top step again. He was not looking at her hands this time. He was looking at the flag on the referee's table.

If they had bought a meet reserve, she would have had half a dram more. She would have gone out for the third carry with the bar on her fist and all three doors to hand, and done precisely what she had done in the first two, for a little longer.

"I'm declaring still," she said. "And I'm not changing. Not once, whatever happens. If the litter goes thirty paces off, then it goes, and I can't chase it. If somebody swings at me, I can't block." She turned to Jude. "So you'd have to be the bar. Pru's the ward. Noa's the legs. I can be the one thing they can't walk through, and that's all I can be."

Jude Talley was quiet for a moment. Then, for the first time that day, he showed the chipped tooth.

"Then we're your other doors," he said. "I've been called worse." He crouched and rubbed out his chalk with the heel of his hand. "Where do you want to be?"

That was a floor question, and she had a floor answer.

"There." She pointed to the foot of their own steps, where Brask had stood. "With my heel on the bottom riser. He used it. It's free. It doesn't cost anything to lean on a step." She thought of Kellan, saying *huh* very softly on another stretch of sand. "They have to bring the litter past me to take it home. Don't carry it to our porch. We can't lift it up two steps with them hanging on. Bring it to me. I'll plant on it. Then they have to come and get it."

"And when they send Moss over to be touched?"

She had been coming to that.

"I need somebody to say something," she said. "When one of them goes down near me. Straight away, before I've had time to go soft. It doesn't matter what, as long as it's got a number in it."

"A number," said Jude.

"She counts," said Noa, as if that explained it. "It's in her tape. I can show you."

"I'll do it," said Pru. She had lifted her head. Her face was blotched and her scarf was gone entirely, and she looked like a person who has been told she may, after all, be useful. "I'm good at counting disasters. I've been doing it under my breath all afternoon."

Down on the sill, the referee turned her glass.

"Third carry," she said. "Doors."

Kiva stood up. The knee held.

"Rowan," she said. "Still."

---

## CHAPTER — books/book-2680-opening/manuscript/chapter-16.md

# Chapter 16 — Kiva: What Carries

The referee's flag stayed on the table.

Kiva saw Lowen Brask notice that. He had come down to the sill for the third carry with his eyes already on the white cloth, as a man looks at the clock he means to catch a train by, and when she said *still* for the second time that afternoon, his calm brown gaze came round to her and rested.

He did not look worried. He looked interested.

"Bell," said the referee.

Noa went. This time Jude went with him, on his own struts, which were shorter and clumsier and dug a trench in the raked sand. They had Old Tom by a pole each before the four still-doors were off their bottom step, and they did not try to lift him. They ran him backward across the western half like two boys dragging a sledge.

Kiva walked to the foot of her own porch.

She had sixteen paces to go, and she took them at her own speed, because nobody on the eastern side was going to arrive anywhere quickly. She turned at the bottom step. She set her right heel against the stone riser, hard, the good leg, and felt the whole weight of the Hall behind it.

"Here," she said.

The litter slid to a halt at her feet in a spray of sand. She bent and took the two head poles, and let herself go down.

The still door opened. The sand took her, and the step took what the sand did not, and Old Tom became part of the floor.

"Thirteen hundredths," said Noa, at her ear, and was gone.

---

They came in a row, the four of them, walking.

It was a long time to stand and watch four people walk. She had done harder things that month. She had knelt with a pan in each hand while a husk went past her shoulder. But this was different, because a husk had not been thinking, and every one of the four faces coming toward her across that sand was working something out.

"Pru," said Jude. "The long-armed one. She's yours. Don't stop her. Make her go round, and when she goes round, go round with her."

"That's not a ward. That's a gate."

"Then be a gate."

Pru's ward came up low and wide and curved, with her weight under it. It rang as Idony Keld reached it. Keld looked for the end, as she had before, and stepped to the right, and the end went with her. She stepped left. The silver swung like a door on a hinge.

"One," said Pru breathlessly. "That's one she hasn't got round. Two. Oh, she doesn't like it. Three."
